Closing a review session now also warns that unsent answers and site changes may be lost, and waits for active filling or visual checks to finish. A focused interface test covers cancellation, active-action blocking and confirmed closure; this change has not been tested on a live employer page.

A read-only qwen3.5:9b comparison on a public Vercel Greenhouse form produced a phone suggestion in the baseline, while the lesson-assisted pass flagged Phone for manual review. Both passes completed without suggesting sensitive fields; CAPTCHA and custom-control walls remained. This single non-randomized pair does not establish a causal effect or general lesson quality. The public page capture returned HTTP 200 with Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 (SHA-256 e711063e7b5d9a85c8aaf1a6cd2af81ab1deaa9ff27df815353b58151428d7c2). Startup passed the resource gate, one sampled free-RAM reading during compilation was 12,498,704 KiB, and the model was unloaded afterward; minimum memory was not measured. An initial test build failed because OpenSSL environment variables were missing; the Windows build helper configured them for the successful retry. No form input, clicks or application submission occurred.

A second read-only comparison on the same public Greenhouse form reversed the order: qwen3.5:9b suggested Phone both with and without the lesson, and neither pass suggested a sensitive or model-flagged field. Combined with the first pair, these observations show variable results and do not establish that the lesson helps or harms. The model was unloaded after the test; no form fields were filled or submitted. The ignored comparison accepts WAYPOINT_LESSON_FIRST for order reversal.

The read-only phone comparison now checks the suggested value against the fictional profile and rejects duplicate phone suggestions. In a third baseline-first run, the baseline suggested the exact fictional number while the lesson-assisted pass flagged Phone for review without a suggestion. This is one further observation, not a causal or general quality estimate. The resource-gated model was unloaded after the run; no fields were filled or submitted.

A rendered Edge check with simulated form endpoints also confirmed that cancelling Close review session sends no close request. Reviewed form lessons are currently included in initial inspection only, not subsequent rescan or navigation model mappings; extending them requires preserving revision checks when a lesson changes during the asynchronous operation.

Form rescans and reviewed navigation now supply bounded, relevant reviewed lessons to model mapping, with the same advisory notice as initial inspection. Selected lesson revisions are rechecked before results are returned; changed, disabled, deleted or unreadable lesson state discards suggestions and requires review. Focused experience and navigation tests and web Clippy passed. The new asynchronous navigation integration has not yet been tested end to end with a local model or a lesson changed during an active navigation.

A focused stale-lesson test now checks that changed, disabled, deleted or unreadable lesson snapshots clear navigation suggestions and add a review notice, while an unchanged snapshot preserves them. This exercises result protection directly; it is not an end-to-end asynchronous browser/model test.

An isolated Windows SQLCipher app, qwen3.5:9b and a public Greenhouse application exercised initial inspection and rescan with a fictional profile and reviewed lesson. Changing the lesson outcome after rescan started produced a completed result with the lesson-context notice, zero suggestions and the stale-lesson review notice. This is one read-only case; no fields were filled or applications submitted. The model was started through the resource gate and unloaded afterward; minimum memory was not measured.

A complementary read-only rescan on the same public form left the fictional lesson unchanged: qwen3.5:9b returned four suggestions, the fallible-context notice was present, and no stale-lesson warning appeared. Their correctness was not independently evaluated. The resource-gated model was unloaded; no fields were filled or submitted. Web Clippy and the focused handoff UI test passed after the changes.

A full Windows web-backend suite passed serially (484 passed, 249 ignored). Two default-parallel runs each failed one different test: candidate inspection in the first, and the reviewed-experience API in the second (the outcome-correction response had no revision). The candidate test passed alone. Shared process-wide task state is a possible source of interference, but the failures have not been isolated to a confirmed cause; default-parallel reliability remains unresolved.

The reviewed-experience API test now shares the form-task test lock with tests that install a process-wide inspection task; the ignored browser task test also uses that lock. This prevents a known overlap in those tests. One subsequent default-parallel web suite passed (484 passed, 249 ignored). This does not establish the cause of the earlier candidate-inspection failure or prove all parallel interference is eliminated.

A second subsequent default-parallel web suite also passed (484 passed, 249 ignored); the earlier candidate-inspection failure was not reproduced or explained by these passes.

In a separate read-only rescan of the same Greenhouse form, the four returned suggestions matched the fictional first name, last name, email and phone exactly. This does not establish that all required fields were handled or that other layouts are correct; several custom controls still needed review. The ignored comparison now checks these fictional values when uniquely labelled fields are suggested. Web Clippy passed; the new assertions have not yet run against the model. The resource-gated model was unloaded and no form was filled or submitted.

A public Lever listing was tested read-only with a fictional profile and a reviewed lesson. One rescan reported lesson context but returned no suggestions; later runs fell back to exact-label mapping and returned the fictional email and phone correctly while Full name and several sensitive/custom questions required review. A local-model status error was previously collapsed into the same message as no eligible model, so the cause of fallback is unknown. Navigation now reports status-check errors explicitly; Web Clippy passed. The first discovered Lever URL returned HTTP 404 despite the crawler success flag and was rejected (capture SHA-256 eb7424c94a494a203d1ce4f11c9dff9a25e8fb69cfce1500c0113b1060e0bcb0). The tested listing returned HTTP 200 (capture SHA-256 d01d0f634cdc6fe1357a36c09f874900a0378a37dafed23209d51fda78e8f364). No fields were filled or submitted; minimum memory was not measured and the test-owned model was unloaded.

A later Lever diagnostic did not start the model: the startup guard observed 14,251 MiB available system memory versus 14,490 MiB required. This does not explain the earlier fallback. A focused test confirms that navigation distinguishes a failed model-status check from no eligible resident model and retains the check error in its review message; it passed without loading a model.

The Lever full-name omission was not caused by its required marker: the fictional profile contained separate first and last names but no explicit full_name. The offline form preview now points users to the full-name profile field when an unambiguous non-sensitive Full name field is present and the profile value is absent. It does not construct or reorder names. A focused fictional test covered the required marker, existing full name, unrelated manager label and sensitive field; the notice has not been retested on the live form.

Two read-only offline inspections of the same public Lever form verified the full-name guidance: a fictional profile with separate names produced the missing-full-name notice, while adding an explicit fictional full name produced one exact Full name suggestion and no missing-value notice. No model was loaded; no fields were filled or submitted.

Initial form inspection now preserves a local-model status-check error in the fallback review message rather than reporting only that no model is loaded. A fictional local-browser test injected a status error and confirmed that the inspection returned the specific reason without filling or submitting. Web Clippy passed.

Form inspection now distinguishes no resident model from multiple resident models, partial GPU residency, over-budget models and incomplete runtime reports. Those cases are surfaced as local-model unavailability reasons without relaxing the resource guard. A focused state-classification test and web Clippy passed; this does not establish which state caused the earlier Lever fallback.

In a later read-only Lever diagnostic, qwen3.5:9b remained 100% GPU-resident before and after the rescan. The app reported an invalid model sensitivity-review batch and conservatively returned no suggestions, marking affected controls for manual review. This explains the zero-suggestion result in that run, not the separate earlier fallback. No fields were filled or submitted; minimum RAM was not measured and the model was unloaded.

An invalid sensitivity-review response now receives bounded repair requests for groups of at most five fields; failed groups remain manual, valid flags from the original response remain in force, and a runtime error stops subsequent repairs and marks remaining fields unclassified. Focused mapping tests passed (31 passed, 13 ignored), plus a runtime-error stop test. In one subsequent read-only Lever rescan, qwen3.5:9b returned correct fictional email and phone suggestions while sensitive/custom fields remained for review; the output does not establish whether the split repair ran or caused the change. The model remained GPU-resident and was unloaded, no fields were filled or submitted, and minimum RAM was not measured. Smaller repairs can add up to four sequential calls per invalid 20-field batch.

Sensitivity-review repair is now capped at four model calls across the entire inspection, rather than four per mapping batch. When the cap is reached, remaining unchecked fields stay in manual review and the preview explains the limit. A 41-field fictional test verified the shared cap and unresolved-field handling; the runtime-error stop test and web Clippy also passed. Large-form latency and memory have not been measured.

The review preview now reports how many smaller sensitivity-review repair attempts returned structurally valid responses. In one read-only Lever rescan, the initial review was incomplete and 3 of 3 smaller attempts were valid; the returned fictional email and phone suggestions were exact, while sensitive and custom questions remained for review. This is one run and valid structure does not prove semantic sensitivity accuracy. The model was fully GPU-resident and unloaded afterward; no fields were filled or submitted, and minimum RAM was not measured. Focused mapping tests passed (33 passed, 13 ignored).

After these mapping and status changes, the Windows web-backend suite passed (489 passed, 249 ignored) and web Clippy passed. Previously observed parallel-test instability remains a limitation; this passing run does not identify its cause.

A public Ashby listing was inspected read-only without a resident model. Crawl4AI's first capture had no substantive text despite HTTP 200; a bounded wait produced a substantive capture (SHA-256 d5f87f4fd6017b8b8ad32835fb15af3fe09788759cad921e6967cc18db7567f8). The form exposed a Passport Country field that was not deterministically marked sensitive. The identity screen now treats passport, nationality and citizenship labels as sensitive; a focused classifier test passed, and a second live read-only inspection marked Passport Country sensitive. CAPTCHA, an obscured custom control and an unstable capture still required review; no field was filled or submitted. This keyword screen is not a complete multilingual classifier.

A read-only qwen3.5:9b inspection and rescan of the public Ashby form returned one suggestion, the fictional email, which matched the profile. Passport Country was marked sensitive, the referrer field was flagged for manual review, and Name plus several other required fields remained unresolved; the fictional profile had no explicit full name. The page reported CAPTCHA and an obscured control. The model was fully GPU-resident and unloaded afterward; no fields were filled or submitted, and minimum RAM was not measured. One run does not establish general Ashby or model accuracy.

A read-only real-browser check of the previously crawled Lever URL confirmed that the browser driver rejects its HTTP 404 before listing extraction. This checks one unavailable URL, not general stale-ad detection; it makes no claim that all closed jobs return 404. The test did not type, click or submit.

An isolated running app also inspected the unavailable Lever listing through its listing-inspection endpoints and returned a failed result explicitly reporting HTTP 404. No job was extracted by that path. A 404 is an unavailable page observation, not proof that the position is closed; no application was submitted.

On the same public Ashby form with an explicit fictional full name, the offline fallback suggested only email and left the ambiguous Name field for review. A separate read-only qwen3.5:9b rescan suggested the exact fictional full name for Name and the exact fictional email, while marking Referrer name and Passport Country for manual review. Other required fields, CAPTCHA and an obscured control remained unresolved. This is one model run and does not establish general name disambiguation. The resource-gated model was fully GPU-resident and unloaded; no fields were filled or submitted, and minimum RAM was not measured.

A public employer-hosted careers page returned HTTP 200 and substantive Crawl4AI text (SHA-256 f867219d0a1e5cb0fdbdaba3096ce75a600c1760096783c5b0653fc5a6d25fd7). Waypoint's hidden-browser inspection and rescan found no fields and reported an obscured control. A separate read-only Edge inspection found 12 controls and one form; the first field had an ancestor at opacity zero until scrolled into view. The preview now suggests trying a visible-browser scroll and rescan when an empty form has this obscured-control warning, without claiming scrolling will resolve it. A fictional guidance test passed; the Waypoint scroll/rescan path has not yet been tested on this employer page. No field was filled or application submitted.

A read-only Waypoint browser-driver check on that employer page scrolled a specifically supplied field selector into view and rescanned: the initially empty schema then contained three fields, while the obscured-control warning remained. This verifies one driver-level recovery, not a complete form inspection or the app's visible-browser handoff; other controls remained undetected. The selector was supplied only to the test. No fields were filled or submitted.

A prototype bounded six-step viewport scroll-and-rescan did not find fields on the same long employer page and added noticeable inspection delay. The experiment was removed, and the app binary was rebuilt without it. Automatic discovery of fields revealed deeper on long pages remains unresolved; the existing visible-browser guidance and driver-level selector test remain.

For an otherwise empty form with an obscured-control warning, Waypoint now attempts one non-activating scroll to a uniquely identified, same-page application anchor, then reinspects. It does not attempt this when a CAPTCHA, login or other hard wall is present, and preserves prior review warnings and a notice that other fields may be outside the inspected area. On the public employer page where blind scrolling failed, one read-only run found five fields and two fictional name suggestions; other fields remained undetected and the page capture remained unstable. Fictional tests cover anchor restrictions and CAPTCHA/login blocking; web Clippy passed. This does not establish complete form discovery or behavior on other sites, and no fields were filled or submitted.

The form-task test group passed (13 passed, 11 ignored), as did all 36 waypoint_applications unit and adapter tests. These checks cover the navigation guards and sensitivity classifier, not complete live form discovery or submission.

A separate read-only Edge experiment on the long employer form found 11 non-file controls with an opacity-zero ancestor initially; after sequentially scrolling each into view, all 11 had no opacity-zero ancestor. This establishes a possible reveal mechanism on one page, not that Waypoint can safely collect or fill all fields across scroll positions. No field was edited or submitted.

The same-page recovery now scrolls through at most 12 ordinary controls inside the unique application section, checks for CAPTCHA/login walls, re-queries control nodes after inspection, and stops for navigation or control-count changes. It never focuses, types, uploads or activates controls. A fictional browser test verified the 12-control cap and another verified refusal of a CAPTCHA wall. In one read-only employer-page run, the app reported scrolling 11 controls and exposed 11 fields; Age and Expected Salary were marked sensitive. At least one form control remained undetected, and capture still reported page changes. This does not establish complete discovery or safe behavior on arbitrary sites; no fields were filled or submitted, and latency was not measured.

The section-reveal path now compares the first post-anchor inspection with the final inspection and warns when previously seen selectors disappear. A fictional test covered the changed and unchanged cases. In a read-only run on the employer page, it reported one earlier field missing after the sweep; the preview remained incomplete and capture remained unstable. Intermediate scroll positions are not included in this comparison, and selector equality does not prove field identity. No data was filled or submitted.

The section-reveal worker now proceeds only when its own inspection reports no walls other than explicit custom-control advisories; unexpected walls stop it as well. It checks again after each group of four scrolls and does not claim immediate detection between checkpoints. The CDP transport caps a protocol message at 16 MiB; this is not a measured whole-process memory bound. Both fictional browser tests and web Clippy passed after the change.

The section-reveal path now clears detected fields if the page URL changes during discovery, and a fictional browser fixture confirmed that a scroll-triggered URL change stops the sweep. Three fictional browser tests and web Clippy passed. One parallel web-suite run again failed the previously intermittent candidate-inspection test (492 passed, 1 failed, 251 ignored); after improving that test's error reporting, the next run passed (493 passed, 251 ignored). The intermittent failure's cause remains unconfirmed.

A fictional browser fixture now verifies that the section sweep stops when the form's control inventory changes during scrolling. The focused browser test passed. A further parallel web-suite run passed (493 passed, 251 ignored), but the earlier intermittent candidate test failure did not reproduce, so its cause remains unresolved.

Section discovery now checks backend node identities and order for up to 12 controls, so replacement or reordering with an unchanged count stops the sweep. A 12-second deadline is checked between operations; an individual protocol call can extend it. Five fictional browser tests passed, including a same-count replacement, and web Clippy passed. A subsequent read-only employer-page run still exposed 11 fields, warned that one previously seen field was absent, and retained the unstable-capture warning. No field was filled or submitted; full discovery remains unproven.

The missing-field warning now includes up to three bounded field labels to help locate them, without restoring their old selectors as current suggestions. The focused test passed. In a read-only employer-page run, the notice identified the missing application-text prompt; the page remained incomplete and capture was unstable. No form input or submission occurred.

After the section-discovery and missing-field changes, web Clippy passed and the parallel web-backend suite passed (493 passed, 251 ignored). The previous intermittent candidate-test failure remains unexplained; this passing run does not prove it fixed.

A missing-field observation now persists as a historical manual-review obligation across rescans and navigation within the browser session; it does not assert that the field is still absent. A focused test checked propagation and deduplication. A read-only rescan of the employer page retained the warning after the initial inspection identified a missing application-text field. The page remained incomplete and its capture unstable; no data was filled or submitted.

Capture events from the employer page showed width alternating between 1,243 and 1,258 CSS pixels while height remained 6,694 pixels. An experimental increase in the post-scroll settling delay from 500 to 1,200 ms still resulted in changing dimensions during both captures, so the delay change was reverted. The capture continues to report incomplete evidence instead of treating the dimensions as equivalent. One experiment does not establish the cause of the width changes; no application was submitted.

A separate read-only Edge/CDP experiment scrolled the same employer form and captured four 2,000-pixel tiles. In that Playwright-managed context, reported page width and height stayed constant after each capture at both tested viewport configurations. This does not reproduce the app's width alternation, does not prove capture correctness, and differs from the app's browser setup; the underlying cause remains open. No application data was entered or sent.

Waypoint's own CDP driver reproduced the width change after the bottom screenshot tile: 1,243 to 1,258 CSS pixels with unchanged height, returning to 1,243 at the first 100 ms sample in one run. This suggests a transient capture or page effect but does not establish which or prove the screenshot geometry is correct. The strict capture check is unchanged; no fields were filled or submitted.

An overlapping 2,000-pixel bottom tile kept the measured page dimensions unchanged in a direct browser-driver probe. The application capture planner now uses an overlapping full-height final tile when possible, recording its actual coordinates and retaining the same six-tile limit and strict dimension/first-tile checks. The tile-planning test passed. In two read-only runs on the employer page, dimensions stayed unchanged but the first tile differed on recheck, so both captures remained incomplete; no reliable full-page capture is claimed. Overlap can increase captured image data, and latency or peak memory was not measured. No fields were filled or submitted.

For one incomplete capture, the first and rechecked tiles had equal dimensions but differed at least at 86,125 of 2,486,000 pixels under a grayscale difference check (bounding box x=0..1242, y=105..832). Visual inspection showed differences in the page's hero background. This is evidence of visible content change, not merely PNG encoding, so the capture is correctly left incomplete. It does not identify the site's underlying animation mechanism. No user data or application was submitted.

Inspection of one isolated employer-page attempt record confirmed that, despite the incomplete full-page capture, its viewport PNG, field-inspection event, four partial tile events and explicit incomplete-capture event were retained. This confirms artifact presence for that run only; it does not establish coverage of every field or visual correctness.

The browser fixture now checks capture segments by capture ID, including retained segments from retries. After an edit it accepts either a complete capture or an explicitly incomplete one and checks the saved segment files in both cases. The offscreen, changing-page and narrow-page browser fixtures passed, as did web Clippy and formatting. A transparent-caret experiment did not eliminate the intermittent page-change result; its cause remains unverified.

The form review now offers a user-initiated batch of up to 12 focused captures of filled fields. It stops on a missing record, changed page, unverified value, expired session, or closed panel, and identifies the saved count and limit. Each view remains historical and may omit or obscure the field; no full coverage or visual correctness is claimed. Focused JavaScript tests cover the limit and stopping cases. The real-browser focus fixture initially failed because its manually filled values were absent from the reviewed-answer mapping; after correcting that fixture, its existing checks for retained values, scrolling changes, and animated-page changes passed. Web Clippy passed. This does not establish the batch's behavior on a third-party form.

The batch button's handler was also exercised with a simulated two-field response: a saved second capture with an unverified value was counted as saved, the warning was displayed, and controls were re-enabled. This is a simulated UI test, not rendered-browser or third-party validation.

Batch capture now starts from the selected filled field and, after a successful 12-view batch, selects the next field so a further user action can cover longer forms. An uncertain batch does not advance the selection. A simulated 15-field interaction verified the 12-plus-3 sequence and that a failed value check leaves the original selection in place. The captures remain historical and coverage of the actual field contents is not guaranteed.

An isolated in-app browser fixture using the current batch handler and simulated capture responses displayed 12 of 15 saved, selected fictional field 13, then displayed 3 of 3 saved on a second click. This exercised real browser DOM interactions but did not run the complete application or save screenshots; third-party behavior remains unverified.

Focused field navigation now checks tracked attachments after scrolling, using the same eight-group and four-second limits as control navigation. Failed or unverified attachments mark the attempt for manual review, appear in capture events and responses, and stop batch capture. The local browser fixture confirmed saved captures for two different fields and reported a deliberately missing staged file; a JavaScript test confirmed the batch stops on that failure. Web Clippy passed. Attachment retention on a real employer site has not been established.

The same local browser fixture now selects a fictional file in an actual file input and confirms that the focused check reports it retained; after the test removes its staged copy, the check reports one failed attachment. This does not establish behavior for employer-side uploads or files cleared by a site.

The local fixture also cleared the file input while the staged file still existed. The next focused check reported a failed attachment, and the attempt record retained the corresponding capture. This exercises a native file input in a local fixture; custom and remote employer upload widgets remain unverified.

Structured listing deadlines now show a review cue when a current-URL capture states a deadline that has passed. Date-only values receive a conservative 48-hour window from UTC midnight; timestamps must include a timezone, and malformed values do not trigger the cue. The listing is not automatically removed or classified closed. Focused interface tests cover offsets, invalid dates, the date-only boundary and captures from another URL; employer data and the system clock can still be wrong.

For a newly saved structured listing, the same advisory also appears in the single-listing notification or selected-listings inspection log. It is suppressed for an already-saved listing and model-extracted job details because the result may not describe the saved job's current evidence. Focused date and inspection-queue simulations passed; this does not verify employer deadlines or establish that a role is closed.

A fictional inspection API test confirmed that the result carries the deadline and distinguishes a new listing from a reinspection; a simulated 100-item queue confirmed the advisory appears in its log. These checks do not validate provider data or the complete rendered workflow.

An isolated browser fixture rendered the saved-listing date component with fictional expired dates. The matching source showed the advisory; the mismatched source showed a source warning without the deadline advisory, and both remained visible. This was a component fixture, not the complete app or an employer-page check.

On 2026-11-01, the local qwen3.5:9b search planner was checked with a fictional Rust qualification and remote Berlin/Paris preferences. The original prompt produced repeated variants and omitted “remote” from five of ten phrases. A revised prompt asked for distinct roles and the remote requirement; its first run introduced unsupported seniority. The final prompt also asked not to assume seniority: one run returned ten different role phrases, all containing “remote” and none containing “senior”. Specialty terms such as embedded and security were suggested from minimal Rust evidence and may not suit the candidate, so review remains necessary. The model was started through the resource gate and unloaded afterward; one observed available-memory sample during use was about 10.96 GiB, not a measured minimum. This is a small prompt comparison, not evidence of general accuracy or recall.

Two further fictional qwen3.5:9b profiles exposed limits: a laboratory example omitted its requested on-site arrangement, while an accounting example added junior and senior roles. After an explicit seniority instruction, one run avoided those terms but still suggested unsupported specialties. Available RAM later sampled about 9.66 GiB and the test-owned model was unloaded; samples do not establish the minimum. To preserve broader search recall, the prompt was subsequently changed to request a mix of arrangement-specific and broader phrases, and the review interface explicitly warns about inferred specialties, seniority and omitted preferences. At that point the final prompt revision had not yet been live-model tested; search phrases do not establish eligibility.

Following that memory observation, the model startup estimate now requires available system RAM of at least the model's disk size plus 10 GiB, increased from 8 GiB. Synthetic boundary tests passed and the model inventory explains the headroom. This is still a point-in-time estimate: other processes can consume memory after the check, and it cannot guarantee a minimum or prevent freezes. The stricter estimate may refuse a model that could otherwise load.

The inventory and startup guard were checked at the same fictional 16 GiB boundary for a 6 GiB model, and the read-only local Ollama inventory test passed. At a later observation only about 14.9 GiB of system RAM was available with no resident model, below the 9B model's new startup estimate; it was not loaded, so the latest planning prompt still lacks live qualification.

After an interrupted autofill, the review page now recovers completed field selectors from the matching active session and offers them for focused inspection. A different or unavailable session does not expose or reuse those selectors; recovered commands are not treated as evidence that current values are correct. A fictional browser fixture verified matching and mismatched session responses after a partial fill, and a UI simulation covered successful recovery and session changes. This has not been tested on an employer form.

If a recovered completed selector is absent from the displayed review, the interface now reports how many fields need manual inspection instead of silently leaving them out. A simulated interruption covered a recovered field plus one unmatched selector. This does not establish that the displayed fields remain correct.

Lesson candidates for form mapping are now drawn from the bounded enabled inventory, then ranked after inspection using the observed page origin rather than the listing's source category. Recognized hosts only provide a weak ordering hint; unknown or custom hosts do not receive a platform match. Selection retains at most five diverse text and five diverse choice observations, and only when a corresponding non-sensitive control exists. Tests covered a Lever page with competing Greenhouse observations, a deceptive lookalike host, unknown hosts and separate control budgets; web Clippy passed. The top-level origin may not identify an embedded form's platform, and no live-model or employer-site effect was measured.

The web backend suite passed with two test threads (494 passed, 253 ignored). A subsequent fictional 500-lesson test confirmed that selection still passes at most five observations per present control class and can prioritize an older matching-host observation. This does not measure runtime on a populated production workspace or prove that a lesson improves model decisions.

Inspection and navigation now retain revision dependencies for the selected lessons when a mapping request actually included hints, rather than all candidates read before the page was known. A changed or revoked selected lesson still invalidates suggestions; an unrelated lesson change does not. A fictional local-browser fallback test confirmed that an inspection which did not use the model retained no lesson dependencies. This tracking is conservative within selected control groups and does not prove that the model used the hints correctly.

After the freshness-tracking changes, the web backend suite again passed with two threads (494 passed, 253 ignored). The ignored live-model and employer-site tests were not run.

Search-plan review now flags phrases made from the same whitespace-separated terms in a different order, while keeping every phrase selectable and noting that search engines may treat order differently. A simulated interface test covered reordered phrases and kept C++ and C# distinct. This is a narrow review aid, not semantic deduplication or evidence that phrases return the same results.

On 2026-11-01, the final search-planner prompt was exercised once each against fictional Rust, laboratory and accounting profiles with qwen3.5:9b. Both live tests passed; the Rust and laboratory results mixed arrangement-specific and broader phrases, while some specializations still went beyond the supplied facts. A fictional browser inspection with one reviewed lesson also passed and recorded the lesson revision. These runs do not establish general accuracy. The model was started through the resource gate and unloaded; one available-memory sample during the run was about 14.9 GiB, not a measured minimum.

A read-only inspection of a public Workable application on 2026-11-01 found five ordinary fields, an incomplete custom-choice inventory, an obscured-field warning, required custom controls and a CAPTCHA. A qwen3.5:9b mapping preview completed with fictional profile data; it flagged invalid or duplicate suggestions and left sensitive fields for review. No values were entered and no application was submitted. The bounded Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 capture returned HTTP 200 with 7,425 markdown bytes (SHA-256 1daded31f8b4e5e4409781146de1a3f25100ab9b637e1f430011d25cb91c0c20; native result SHA-256 691f8d871480c53500d376ee6d66fb638bd3aa4df5aafb2c772872ef667150e0) in the local temporary directory. This single page does not prove successful filling, visual correctness or general platform coverage.

A follow-up read-only mapping run on the same form showed qwen3.5:9b proposing the fictional city for the whole address and an empty profile key for a cover letter; validation withheld both. An experimental prompt explicitly distinguishing complete addresses and empty keys still proposed city for address and instead flagged the optional cover letter for review. That prompt change was discarded. The model was unloaded; no fields were filled or submitted. These individual runs do not establish how often the errors occur.

Postal autocomplete tokens now reject incompatible model-proposed profile components even when the field label is unfamiliar. A fictional browser fixture with a German label and a scripted city-for-street response confirmed that the field remained empty and the missing required suggestion was reported. This is not a live-model measurement; autocomplete tokens can be wrong, and matching a token does not prove a value is complete or belongs to the applicant.

On 2026-11-01, a separate fictional multilingual browser test used resource-gated qwen3.5:9b at a 4,096-token context. It mapped and filled the given-name and email fields, left the fixture's sensitive fields blank, and its visual transcription matched those two entered values. Ollama reported the model as 100% GPU resident; available host memory was sampled at approximately 14.6 GiB after the test, not continuously measured. The model was unloaded afterward. This one fixture does not validate postal mapping, employer-site behavior or general visual accuracy.

In a separate resource-gated run on 2026-11-01, qwen3.5:9b mapped both a fictional German street-address and city field correctly when both profile values were available. The local browser was inspected without entering values. Ollama reported 100% GPU residency; available memory sampled after the run was approximately 14.8 GiB, not a measured minimum. The model was unloaded afterward. This single positive observation does not measure missing-address behavior or generalize to real applications.

A subsequent run of that fictional fixture included both the complete profile and a profile with only a city. With the incomplete profile, qwen3.5:9b marked the street field for manual review and suggested neither field, including the available city; the application reported two required fields without suggestions. With both values present it mapped both correctly. No fields were filled. Ollama reported the model as 100% GPU resident, and one post-test host-memory sample was approximately 15.1 GiB; the model was unloaded. These two cases do not establish a success rate or real-site behavior.

After adding a general instruction to consider each field independently, one repeat on the same fictional fixture mapped the available city and omitted the missing street; with both values present it mapped both. Separate fictional multilingual and applicant-versus-referee contact tests also passed once. These are individual comparisons, not a measured improvement rate or evidence of employer-site reliability. Ollama reported the model as 100% GPU resident; a post-test available-memory sample was approximately 14.9 GiB, not a measured minimum. The model was unloaded.

Form text areas now offer an explicit control to load an original saved cover-letter draft for the current job, review or edit it, and copy it into the proposed answer. The draft's context is checked when loaded and again before use; copying does not fill the browser. The answer text is recorded when filling, but this route does not link the answer back to the saved draft, and PDF export edits are not recovered. A simulated UI test covered selection, stale-context rejection and copying; this has not been visually tested in a rendered interface or on an employer form.

A standalone rendered fixture subsequently exercised that control in headless Edge at 1200×900 and 390×844, using mocked draft responses. It loaded and copied fictional text without page errors; the narrow viewport had no horizontal overflow. The layout was adjusted after inspection, and a simulated delayed response confirmed that copying does not overwrite an answer edited while the check is in flight. The full application and an employer form were not exercised in this check.

Copied cover-letter answers now carry the saved draft identifier through the form review request. Before filling, the backend requires that the draft belongs to the current job and matches the current document context; the answer record receives generated and reviewed text hashes, an edited flag, and the bounded draft evidence. Evidence remains advisory and describes the original generated text rather than later edits. Focused backend and simulated UI checks passed, and a fictional browser endpoint fixture exercised provenance attachment for an edited answer. PDF export edits still cannot be recovered through this control; this has not been tested on an employer form.

The fictional reviewed-fill endpoint fixture was extended to check the written answer snapshot itself: its field record contained the selected cover-letter draft ID and an edited flag. The test used a synthetic text value and a local browser; it did not submit an application or establish behavior on an employer site.

The answer-history panel now indicates when a recorded answer came from a saved cover-letter draft and whether it was edited, with a reminder that draft evidence applies to the original text. A simulated history and paging test passed; this display has not been inspected in the full rendered application.

The form's saved-letter control now lets the user explicitly remove its draft link while preserving the answer, so a stale link need not prevent using independently reviewed text. The resulting answer is recorded without that draft evidence. Simulated checks covered unlinking and an in-flight read that must not restore the removed link; the standalone rendered fixture passed at desktop and narrow widths. This does not make the answer accurate or test an employer form.

Batch web search now stores only its task ID and an empty recovery cursor in browser session storage instead of repeatedly serializing all result pages. On recovery, the existing backend history replay restores the visible groups. If browser storage is unavailable, polling continues and the interface warns that backend recovery may still be available. Simulated checks covered storage quota errors and history replay; this has not been measured on a large live search or through a full application restart.

The recovery path also checks backend history if browser storage cannot be read. A focused HTTP endpoint test verified that a zero-cursor client receives both previously processed pages while the batch is still active; simulated UI tests covered unavailable browser storage. This is not a full application restart or a live-provider test.

Stopping an active batch no longer depends on reading browser session storage: the UI uses the ID of the task it is currently following. Simulated checks covered a storage failure, cancellation of the active task and no cancellation when none is active. Cancellation still waits for an in-flight provider request to finish and has not been exercised here against a live search provider.

On 2026-11-02, the Wiby adapter was exercised with three generic queries. For software engineering, laboratory science and electrical engineering in Switzerland respectively, it reported 12, 2 and 2 provider results, of which 9, 2 and 1 were usable HTTPS candidates. None of the candidate titles contained the English fragments job, career, vacanc or hiring; that simple title count does not determine whether a page contains a vacancy. No candidate pages were opened, and the results remain unverified. These queries show limited usefulness in this small sample, not overall provider coverage or a live application search.

The SearXNG search API documentation notes that JSON must be enabled in the instance settings and many public instances disable it. The 403 message now explains disabled JSON and restricted access as possibilities and suggests checking configuration or another provider, without asserting which caused the rejection. A fictional HTTP 403 test and the web-search unit group passed. On 2026-11-02, the official documentation was captured with Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2, HTTP 200, markdown SHA-256 0bd4efcfd85d9851ded25b4e161ebdf146dafff2f6e1de6fed699624733b13de, native capture SHA-256 afa7c47dad35e14c2f28fd838c8505c5d0aeb940b1b791cc455baab813b4e311. This does not prove any particular public instance permits JSON or meets the user's needs.

Two public SearXNG instance homepages were checked on 2026-11-02 with the development crawler; both were denied by their robots rules, so no search queries were sent to either instance. Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 classified `https://search.jeremyh.xyz/` and `https://searxng.deggo.fyi/` as `robots_denied`; their retained native captures have SHA-256 c25db15dd8d5469de86376b5749f38a5c1b472902f2b0970d4d3b5d111d7ee8a and f20bd060fa36bd23207453f1bac3af2ac9e3656b55a67823c1cf0fff8b4233b4 respectively. These homepage checks do not establish the policies of other instances.

On 2026-11-02, two additional search APIs were researched but not integrated. Purili's developer page describes a no-key API, while its terms restrict the service to personal, non-commercial use; this is unsuitable as an unrestricted enterprise default. Freesea describes a free, sponsored search API but requires an account-created key and has shared capacity limits; terms and sponsored-result handling would need further review. No queries were sent to either service. Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 returned HTTP 200 for the Purili developer page, API documentation and terms (native capture SHA-256 9bf0028de54c53f78eb75003381a76f9b61c4f9591effb2505d77caf3a016e65, 7dafb5cbb2522499ae18fdc185556309f264234df9b77d7bb94973cb3c1634fd and b0be84b0c26c88e8ee265590fb669f082265f0fad21bb1db23b59d08aa4859b3), and for the Freesea overview and limits (2a261d9d9eb22c69e5ad788c726ecd7b65c9e8105da62ae42b3fe0647eaa9953 and 08ec1a8d527d2b1a8f4774328a844e476520ec54411f7075a59fb9d67ad609b6). These are point-in-time provider claims, not endorsements or guarantees.

SmartRecruiters' official Posting API documentation describes public company-specific listings and search parameters, but presents the integration as a customer career-site capability. The current permission registry marks SmartRecruiters discovery and reading as requiring review; third-party use and applicable terms were not established in this pass, so no adapter or live posting request was added. On 2026-11-02, Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 captured its endpoint and overview pages with HTTP 200 (native SHA-256 0437e254ad455baca8a506bdecaeb6971aea9ad47b30c58cf91b0ddd0167d1cc and 8bfed8a7078ec7d0918f20dec363dded6cd6b4f6ab8ce5bfb4f74d05b132a944). Public technical accessibility alone does not establish permission to aggregate or redistribute listings.

On 2026-11-02, a read-only browser inspection of a public DoorDash Greenhouse application found 29 ordinary fields, 27 required, and 14 custom-choice controls with incomplete option inventories. The driver reported a CAPTCHA, an embedded-frame warning, required controls needing manual review and a changing page. No values were entered and no application was submitted. The preceding Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 capture returned HTTP 200 (markdown SHA-256 62bb961570f28f9474994908543d4a2db56c7165dbf29a52449b38cd0c8f47b1; native capture SHA-256 c597102bd30c3b67bc7d1a851b26f97a9f1bc245ceb39a13803f574bb2e8f6e4). This inspection does not establish complete field detection or autofill capability on the site.

Custom-choice review now pages through all controls returned by the bounded inventory, 15 at a time, instead of silently showing only the first 15. Changing pages reinspects the inventory and retains the existing visual and review gates. A simulated 31-control test covered forward and backward navigation; the inventory can still be incomplete, option lists can be partial, and this has not been tested on the DoorDash form or in the full rendered interface.

A standalone headless Edge fixture subsequently rendered 31 fictional choices and navigated forward and backward at 1200×900 and 390×844. Each page change requested a fresh mocked inventory; reload returned to the first page. No page errors or narrow-screen horizontal overflow were observed. This did not exercise the full app, live model, custom-choice actions or an employer site.

The real browser inventory was subsequently found to cap candidates at 15, making later UI pages unreachable. Its bounded cap is now 45, retaining the existing four-second between-control deadline and incomplete flag. A local browser fixture with 46 fictional controls returned 45, including controls 16 and 45, and reported the cap as incomplete. This one run does not guarantee all 45 will be inspected within the deadline on slower machines or establish employer-site behavior.

The web backend's `custom_choice_snapshot` path was also exercised with the same fictional browser fixture. It retained 45 controls, the incomplete flag and a snapshot digest without activating controls. This checks the backend snapshot used by the endpoint, not the full HTTP/UI interaction or an employer page.

After the custom-choice cap change, read-only inspection of the same public Greenhouse form returned 21 custom controls, all with incomplete option inventories, versus 14 in the earlier observation. In a separate repeat the initial inspection did not report a CAPTCHA, while a subsequent inspection after reading the choice inventory did. The page is dynamic, so this is not an isolated measurement of the cap change and absence from one snapshot is not evidence that no CAPTCHA exists. The fill path reinspects the page before action, but delayed changes can still occur; no fields were entered or submitted.

Initial form inspection now performs a final read-only inspection before returning its review. Newly observed walls are retained, while a changed URL, origin or form schema causes the inspection to fail for manual retry instead of presenting mappings from the earlier page. The existing pre-fill checks remain necessary because a page can still change afterward. Focused merge tests, the form-task unit group and one fictional local-browser worker test passed. The change has not been measured for latency or verified against the live Greenhouse form.

Web Clippy passed after correcting a test-only lint in the earlier provider-error test.

On 2026-11-02, the actual background inspection worker was exercised read-only on the public DoorDash Greenhouse page with no model loaded. The final check reported that the application changed during review preparation and returned no form preview. This avoids presenting that stale mapping, but makes the dynamic form unavailable through this run. No fields were entered and no application was submitted. The test accepts a reported change or final-check failure as an observed outcome; it does not prove that the challenge was detected or that the form can be completed.

When the final form schema changes on the same URL and origin, the worker now returns the latest field inventory as an explicitly informational preview, discards earlier suggestions and blocks filling until a fresh inspection. Initial walls are retained; a changed URL or origin still fails the inspection. Focused tests covered the changed-schema block in backend and UI, and web Clippy passed. Two subsequent read-only runs of the same public form returned 29 fields and a CAPTCHA warning; in the run that recorded the informational flag, the schema did not change and the new branch was not exercised. No fields were entered or submitted.

A fictional local-model worker experiment reached the changed-schema branch once, retaining the added CAPTCHA and clearing suggestions, but a repeat finished before its timer changed the page. The timing-dependent fixture was removed rather than treated as reliable. That experiment exposed a misleading fallback message and missing required-field warnings after suggestions were cleared; both were corrected and covered by a deterministic test. The model was unloaded. The corrected branch has not been re-exercised with a live model.

The corrected merge path was subsequently tested with a real browser and resource-gated qwen3.5:9b on a fictional form. After a completed local-model response, the test explicitly changed the fixture, then reinspected it: the resulting informational preview retained the new CAPTCHA, removed suggestions, reported all three required fields and blocked reuse through the review-wall check. This invokes the merge path directly rather than the background worker and does not establish employer-site behavior. Ollama reported the model as 100% GPU resident; available host memory was sampled at approximately 14.8 GiB afterward, not continuously measured. The model was unloaded.

On 2026-11-02, the background inspection worker visited a public Spotify Lever application read-only with an empty fictional profile and no model. Two runs returned 25 fields and a CAPTCHA warning; the second reported 8 required fields, 0 suggestions and 13 review warnings. The Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 capture returned HTTP 200 (markdown SHA-256 f272e5ef241050adfb5e00a39ffd0d4f4729a3c2743fadd905ef4b48f2d8c581; native capture SHA-256 44fbf07311d4656bcbdce2279a26eb4c8509a19e3a167e5158d7b0b262f527c1). No data was entered or application submitted. This does not prove completeness, model mapping, or successful form filling.

A read-only inspection of the public Spotify Lever form exposed three checkbox options without their shared visible question. Form inspection now attempts bounded nearby-question discovery for same-name checkbox groups, marks inferred questions as advisory and the corresponding options sensitive, and leaves ambiguous or hidden context unassociated. A fictional browser fixture and the existing radio-group fixture passed; browser Clippy passed. A repeat read-only inspection recovered the question for all three options on that page; no values were entered or submitted. The shared 16-question inspection budget and heuristic do not cover every layout, and model mapping was not evaluated because available system RAM was below the startup reserve.

For a detected same-name checkbox group, an ambiguous or hidden nearby question now produces an explicit unavailable-context label and marks its options sensitive for review. Fictional browser fixtures covered ambiguous and hidden text as well as a recoverable question; the existing radio-group fixture also passed. Detection remains bounded and cannot establish context for all forms.

On 2026-11-02, a resource-gated qwen3.5:9b inspection of the public Spotify Lever form initially returned deterministic fallback suggestions because same-name checkboxes shared selectors; an explicit no-fallback assertion exposed this. Duplicate checkbox selectors now use existing structural paths, and ambiguous-control walls may produce advisory suggestions while retaining backend and interface fill blocks. A fictional checkbox fixture, radio fixture, advisory guard tests and web Clippy passed. One subsequent read-only model run returned three suggestions labelled Full name, Email and Phone without the fallback warning; it flagged two other fields for review. The CAPTCHA remained and no values were entered or sent. This run did not independently validate suggestion values or form completeness. The model was unloaded, and available system memory sampled afterward was about 20.4 GiB; peak usage was not measured.

On 2026-11-02, two further read-only qwen3.5:9b runs on the public Spotify Lever application produced name, email and phone suggestions matching the fictional profile exactly, with no deterministic fallback warning. The CAPTCHA remained, and no values were entered or submitted. One additional Custom checkbox was flagged for review by the model, but its parent question was not deterministically associated; this remains an open form-understanding gap. Separately, controls beyond the bounded checkbox-context inspection budget are now explicitly marked for review and sensitive, verified on a fictional 17-checkbox browser fixture. The model was unloaded; this small sample does not establish general accuracy or peak memory.

Unnamed checkboxes beside at least two visible same-name checkbox choices in the same form are now marked as having unavailable question context, without inferring a question from nearby descriptive text. A fictional fixture covered that pattern and rejected a mixed-name group. In a subsequent read-only Spotify Lever inspection the previously unassociated Custom choice was marked sensitive for review. The heuristic remains bounded and is not a general semantic association; no fields were filled or submitted.

Form inspection now uses structural selectors for repeated non-radio control selectors, extending the earlier checkbox handling to text and other controls. A fictional browser fixture with repeated email names and phone IDs verified that filling each applicant field left the neighboring reference field empty. Existing unlabelled checkbox and radio fixtures and browser Clippy passed. Structural paths can change when a page changes, so existing reinspection and review gates remain necessary; this does not prove identification on arbitrary forms.

On 2026-11-02, resource-gated qwen3.5:9b reviewed a fictional form where applicant and reference email inputs shared a name and applicant and reference phone inputs shared an ID. In one run it suggested the two applicant fields with the exact fictional profile values and omitted both reference fields. No browser values were entered; the model was unloaded. The labels explicitly identified applicant and reference, so this does not establish accuracy for unlabeled or more complex layouts.

On 2026-11-02, an Ashby-hosted Coder application exposed a local-model error: qwen3.5:9b suggested the fictional legal name for an optional preferred-name field when no preferred name was provided. The profile now has a distinct optional preferred name, and mapping validation refuses substitution between it and legal/first/last names; the prompt also describes the distinction. Fictional validation and profile API tests and web Clippy passed. In one follow-up read-only run, the model still proposed the wrong substitution but validation withheld it; in another run with a distinct fictional preferred name provided, all three returned name/email suggestions had the expected values. The CAPTCHA remained; no values were entered or submitted. The model was unloaded. These few runs do not prove general accuracy. The first Ashby listing capture returned insufficient content; a separate Coder listing and application captured substantive content (application markdown SHA-256 066a7d3a8aaf56e86377da1ae73ec41fe2a190d2272221d56d61171ea21ab9ca; native capture SHA-256 a0156ebe14bfee93adfaaa229c9a784c9ea017eeee59e90300004b5295fcd199).

A fictional preferred-name profile submitted through the local API was retained after closing and reopening the workspace. A simulated interface check verified the separate input and summary row. This does not constitute a rendered accessibility or visual-layout review.

A rendered Edge check of the profile editor at 1,200 and 390 pixels used a fictional mocked API to save and reload separate full and preferred names. No horizontal page overflow or JavaScript errors were observed. The narrow summary label column was widened so Preferred name stays on one line. This does not replace the separate real-workspace persistence test or an accessibility observation. The Browser plugin was unavailable; the check used a temporary Playwright Core setup and installed Edge.

A fictional regression check exposed that the preferred-name offline fallback could apply the applicant's value to a reference's preferred name. Both fallback and model-result validation now require a narrowly recognized applicant preferred-name label; unrecognized wording stays for review. The focused test covers reference, manager and mixed applicant/reference labels, and the suggestions unit group and web Clippy passed. This remains a limited label check, not a general guarantee about whose information a form requests.

Form mapping now withholds applicant profile values from labels explicitly naming a reference, referee, manager, supervisor, recruiter or emergency contact, even if the model proposes them or autocomplete hints match. Fictional validation tests and the suggestions unit group passed; one resource-gated qwen3.5:9b run continued to select the applicant email and phone in a labelled repeated-contact fixture. Web Clippy passed and the model was unloaded. This English-label safeguard is incomplete, can require review for unrelated reference wording, and does not prove third-party context is always detected.

A fictional German, French and Spanish contact fixture was evaluated twice with resource-gated qwen3.5:9b. Both runs suggested all three applicant emails and flagged the three reference emails for review. The deterministic third-party check now also recognizes the explicit reference terms used in that fixture; simulated incorrect model mappings were rejected. The model was unloaded. This small fixture does not cover other wording, Unicode variants or languages, and no application was contacted.

Job match badges now disclose whether reviewed qualifications were selected from a larger set, input limits were reached, no reviewed qualifications exist, or coverage metadata is unavailable. This status uses bounded aggregate counts and does not expose qualification text in the batch endpoint. Fictional coverage, API and badge tests passed, as did mocked rendered Edge checks at 1,200 and 390 pixels and web Clippy. The indicator does not establish that model-selected facts are relevant or complete, and the score is unchanged.

Fit-review prioritization now treats selected, limited, absent or unavailable qualification coverage as incomplete input rather than no flagged issue, while retaining higher priority for explicit conflicts and unknown requirements. Simulated sorting tests and mocked rendered Edge checks at 1,200 and 390 pixels passed, including a limited-input case. This is a review order, not an eligibility decision or a measure of assessment accuracy.

A fictional saved-assessment check verified that qualification coverage is recomputed as selected when only one of two reviewed facts was used, becomes stale when a fact is added, and reports limited after reassessment when the new fact exceeds the input limit. The test used a protected temporary workspace; it did not run an AI model or evaluate ranking accuracy.

Local search-plan validation now includes the saved preferred name among protected identifiers. For names of two to five characters it checks whole word sequences, including simple punctuation-separated variants, rather than rejecting unrelated words that merely contain those letters. Fictional planner tests and web Clippy passed. Single-character names, arbitrary aliases and other identifying information may still require user review; this applies to generated plans, not arbitrary manually entered searches.

The local search planner now asks for some general-web job-intent phrases while keeping others broad and avoiding unsupported specialties. In a resource-gated qwen3.5:9b probe, the prior prompt omitted job-intent wording for a fictional Rust profile and introduced architect; an intermediate prompt introduced clinical, biology, diagnostic and quality specialties for a general lab profile. The final prompt passed the fixture's explicit job-intent and unsupported-term checks for lab, Rust and accounting profiles in one run each. Planner unit tests and web Clippy passed; the model was unloaded. These checks do not prove all generated roles are appropriate or establish search-provider recall, and users must review the phrases.

On 2026-11-02, the actual web-search batch worker sent three non-personal planner-style phrases to Wiby's public JSON API. Two runs each reported zero results for microscopy, ten for remote Rust (seven eligible HTTPS links) and one for payroll accounting (no eligible HTTPS link). The seven retained titles did not obviously describe job listings; the pages were not inspected, so their content and application availability remain unknown. All three requests completed without provider errors. This small sample demonstrates a practical limitation of Wiby for these searches, not general web coverage; no applications were contacted or submitted.
# Optional FreeSerp web search — 2026-11-02

A later employer-specific search returned ten candidate links in the rendered
interface. The first [employer-hosted result](https://job-boards.greenhouse.io/unframe/jobs/4943017101)
redirected to a careers page that stated the role was no longer open; the
Crawl4AI capture recorded HTTP 302 then 200 and native SHA-256
`2c526af11b069468757dea915f17a1d1625251921ca69bcca78523ad20f473bd`.
With no local model, Waypoint initially reported only that no structured job was
found. It now also warns when the listing redirected to a different path on the
same site, without displaying query parameters or asserting closure. An
isolated app endpoint check against that URL returned the warning and saved no
job. A subsequent provider query fell back from its web index and was correctly
refused; the direct endpoint check used the previously captured URL. Candidate
tests passed (45 passed, 20 ignored), as did web Clippy. No application was
opened or submitted, and this one case does not measure stale-link coverage.

In a later isolated running-app check, three generic phrases covering data
engineering, museum work and network technicians each returned ten candidate
links through the rendered wider-web form. Visible results included search
pages, courses and unrelated places; the provider did not report engine
availability. These are candidates, not 30 verified vacancies. A university
course result was captured with Crawl4AI (HTTP 200; Markdown SHA-256
`aec3dd165e6b79826ff9816cd642df4e511b2feb1697ffb3d373c96800eeb`). Its
structured data included a Course. When no local model is resident and no
structured job was found, inspection now reports that course metadata is an
advisory non-vacancy signal and requires manual review; it does not save the
page as a job. A repeat through the rendered form showed that message. An
apparent vacancy returned a 403 access challenge to the crawler (native
capture SHA-256 `b2f8b8e55d40f58879cf0babd6204e1b05b390bea8d5efd771ab97da89d61293`)
and was not inspected further. The first UI attempt stopped at a closed
disclosure panel; a second automation attempt hit the app's CSP while using
`waitForFunction`; both were corrected before the successful run. Browser
plugin was unavailable, so local Edge and Playwright Core were used. Candidate
tests passed (44 passed, 20 ignored), as did web Clippy. No model was loaded,
the apparent vacancy was not verified, and no application was submitted.

The wider-web search accepts `https://freeserp.ai/` as an optional provider.
The adapter requests the provider's web index, maps its offset pagination, and
rejects an explicitly reported fallback to the sites index. Returned links are
unverified candidates; the provider's top-100 query limit and potentially stale
index do not represent complete internet coverage. Existing request and candidate
budgets still apply. Provider documentation: https://freeserp.ai/docs.php.

In a bounded direct API sample, three public, fictional-query phrases returned
10 results each. Their titles included apparent individual vacancies and many
career articles or job-board result pages. No result page was inspected in this
sample, so neither vacancy availability nor application accessibility was proven.
The native responses remain in ignored `target/freeserp-quality-*-20261102.json`.
An additional live test through Waypoint's batch worker searched two phrases
over two pages each: all four requests succeeded and retained 40 distinct
candidate links. This did not inspect the returned pages or test the web UI.
An unusable provider response now stops the batch and reports the remaining
pages as unattempted instead of repeating the same failure.
When FreeSerp supplies an index capture date, Waypoint shows it separately from
the provider's page date and retains it with selected search attribution. It is
not evidence that the listing is current or that an application form is open.
An isolated Windows workspace was exercised through the rendered UI on
2026-11-02 using the query `microscopy technician careers`. It displayed ten
unverified candidates and the provider's capture dates. Inspection of one
apparent vacancy returned HTTP 404 and was marked for manual review. A second
returned no structured job and could not use model extraction because no
eligible local model was loaded; it also required manual review. No application
page was reached or submitted. This small sample is not a provider quality
estimate or a demonstration of end-to-end job classification.
In a follow-up on the second result, the app's resource-gated startup loaded
`qwen3.5:9b`; Ollama reported 100% GPU residency and the system reported at least
approximately 13 GiB available memory in the sampled checks. The local model
extracted a candidate listing, which was saved with incomplete-capture and
unverified-details warnings. The application check reported no detected form
fields, flagged a possible login, and required manual review. It did not fill or
submit anything. The model and isolated app process started for the test were
stopped afterward. This is one page and does not establish general accuracy.
Locally extracted listings now receive a separate model assessment of whether
the proposed employer is the hiring organization, backed by a bounded exact
page excerpt when available. Conflict or insufficient evidence adds a specific
manual-review reason. In one fictional local-model test, `qwen3.5:9b` selected
the stated employer; when the initial extraction was forced to name the lab,
the model returned a conflict with the exact employer sentence. The assessment
is advisory and has not been qualified on the real listing or a representative
corpus.
Two additional fictional recruiter cases were tested with the same local model.
When a recruiter was supplied as the employer, the model reported conflict both
when the page explicitly named the client as employer and when the hiring
organization was unnamed. Both outcomes require review; neither establishes
accuracy on other agency or employer pages.

For unstructured listings, the local model can now select an exact, bounded
page excerpt as a possible application deadline. The excerpt is retained with
source provenance and shown for manual verification; it is not interpreted as
a date or used to mark a job closed. A fictional qwen3.5:9b run extracted one
explicit deadline correctly, and parser tests reject invented or oversized
excerpts. This does not establish deadline accuracy or recall on real pages.
In two additional fictional single runs, qwen3.5:9b left the deadline blank on
a rolling-application page with posting and start dates, and selected the
deadline from a page containing all three dates. A deterministic test also
checks persistence and rejects an excerpt outside a selected role section.
In a subsequent isolated real-site check, the page stated an application
deadline of 21 June 2026 and named the University of Oulu. qwen3.5:9b extracted
that organization and selected the date as a possible deadline, while its
separate employer assessment remained uncertain. An earlier saved extraction
named a different organization; reinspection reported the conflicting identity
instead of overwriting it. The model was fully GPU resident and unloaded after
the check. No application was filled or sent. The robots-checked source capture
returned HTTP 200; its local native-result SHA-256 is
`c5b973ca404740fa156f5b17eb6afae0dee65e09737858d577f951921ecd6033`.

An unstructured public training article found by web search was initially
accepted as a vacancy by qwen3.5:9b. A separate page-type check subsequently
flagged it as a possible non-vacancy, but also misclassified fictional vacancy
examples in an intermediate run. The app therefore retains such results for
manual review and never treats this classification as conclusive. One retest
flagged the same article; this is not a measured false-positive or recall rate.
The native Crawl4AI capture is retained locally in `target/` with SHA-256
`27ea6f508a798e4563a0fac06c20245ed957a55f1454f190931814aaab4c1cfb`.
After a page-type prompt revision, one resource-gated qwen3.5:9b run classified
three fictional vacancies as vacancies and flagged that captured article. This
small comparison does not establish general classification accuracy; an
unrecognized or failed classification remains uncertain, and flagged pages
remain available for review rather than being silently discarded.
In a rendered isolated-workspace run, the actual app inspected that same public
article using qwen3.5:9b. Its initial extraction again saved an article title as
a job, but the job card showed the explicit possible-non-vacancy review reason.
This confirms the warning reached the UI in one real-page case; it does not
prevent misleading candidates or establish general classification accuracy.
The isolated app and the model started for the check were stopped afterward.
Batch inspection now skips the automatic application-page check for a result
that the local model flags as a possible non-vacancy, records the reason, and
continues with the remaining selected links. The candidate remains available
for review because the classification can be wrong. A simulated queue test
covered one flagged result followed by an ordinary candidate. A later rendered
two-page real-site run flagged the training article and skipped its application
check, then also flagged the university vacancy and skipped that check. The
university page's stated deadline had passed, but it was still a job posting;
the non-vacancy classification is therefore a false positive for page type.
Both remained saved for review; neither was filled or submitted. This one run
does not establish general classifier accuracy. The test model and app were
stopped afterward.
After a further prompt revision distinguishing page type from expiry, the model
still classified the captured university job ad as `not_vacancy` in one direct
text test; a separate attempt failed listing extraction before classification.
The app therefore no longer skips a requested application-page check solely on
this model flag. It keeps the warning and preserves the user's selection. The
failed real-text check remains an ignored, opt-in test; the error may depend on
the captured text representation, and no general accuracy claim follows.
A further source-quoted second classification pass also failed to resolve the
same false positive in one local-model run, and was removed to avoid an extra
inference call without demonstrated benefit. The opt-in real-text regression
continues to fail and needs investigation; no automatic route-check skip is
based on this classification.
One diagnostic run classified the complete captured university page and its
first 4,000 bytes as non-vacancy, but its last 4,000 bytes as a vacancy. For
long pages initially classified as non-vacancies, Waypoint now checks the last
4,000 bytes once and reports `uncertain` when those judgments conflict. A
subsequent local-model run returned uncertain for that captured job ad and
continued to flag the captured training article. This adds one bounded model
call to long pages initially judged non-vacancies; the small sample does not
establish general accuracy, causation, or behavior on all page layouts.

### Listing capture stability follow-up (2026-11-02)

The two saved public-page captures in the isolated batch workspace each stopped after one screenshot segment because the page dimensions changed during capture (1243 × 3313 and 1243 × 3523 CSS pixels at the initial measurement). These are observations from those attempts, not a measurement of retry effectiveness. Capture now retries once after 250 ms only for this dimension-change condition; the retry repeats the same address, dimensions and first-tile checks, discards the first attempt's segments, and remains explicitly incomplete if the second attempt fails. The batch UI displays a bounded reason for incomplete captures, including when a previously saved capture is retained. A local browser fixture and focused retry and diagnostic tests passed. The changed retry has not yet been evaluated against those live pages.

The 2026-11-02 read-only retest of the same two public pages produced complete three-segment captures on the second attempt in both cases. This is a two-page observation, not a general success rate. These retest images were not saved: existing first captures are preserved by the current store. Re-inspecting an existing listing likewise does not replace or supplement its first capture; the batch UI now explicitly reports when a complete later capture was discarded. Retaining both versions and including them in application records remains unfinished. The focused candidate-task suite passed 34 tests (13 ignored), and the batch UI check passed.

### Follow-up listing captures (2026-11-02)

Migration 44 adds space for one DPAPI-sealed complete follow-up capture alongside an incomplete first capture. The requested and reached URLs must match the first capture; neither the first capture nor an existing follow-up is overwritten. New attempt folders include both `original-job-page` and, when available, `followup-job-page`. Existing attempt folders remain unchanged. The existing isolated encrypted workspace migrated and retained its first captures; the store suite passed 91 tests (2 ignored), and focused attempt-archive and batch-UI checks passed. The earlier real-page retest images were not saved, and the new persistence path has so far been tested with fictional captures rather than another live-page run.

The fictional listing-inspection status flow now verifies that a complete reinspection is saved as a follow-up while the original capture remains unchanged. Attempt history checks whether the follow-up capture files are present and warns if they are missing or inaccessible; this checks file presence, not image integrity. Focused save-path, archive, inventory and three UI tests passed on 2026-11-02.

A further 2026-11-02 read-only check re-captured the same two previously researched public pages. Each completed on its second capture attempt, was saved as a three-segment follow-up in the isolated encrypted workspace, and matched the captured bytes when read back. The original incomplete captures remained unchanged. Reopening the workspace in a separate test process returned both follow-up metadata records and all six decrypted images. This directly exercises browser capture and storage; the HTTP status route and attempt-folder export were exercised separately with fictional data. No application was sent.

Checkpoint validation on 2026-11-02: the full local web-app Rust suite passed 512 tests (268 ignored), the web-app Clippy check passed, all 124 JavaScript UI tests passed, and formatting and diff-whitespace checks passed. The separate store suite passed 91 tests (2 ignored). These checks do not establish general live-site or model reliability.

### Conflicting experience retrieval (2026-11-02)
