When selecting a contradictory pair for a report on a known platform, retrieval now prefers a counter-report from that same platform before one from another platform. This matters when the selected report is not from the current platform: current-platform reports were already sorted first, so the earlier description overstated the change for them. Cross-platform pairs remain a fallback. A corrected fictional regression covers reports from two non-current platforms: it failed with the earlier selection rule and passed with the new one. The workspace experience group passed (9 tests). No live-model comparison was run, so its effect on model decisions remains unmeasured.

The lesson lookup and the production form-inspection path now share the same bounded selector. When reports for the same observation and response include both `helped` and `did_not_help`, one such pair is retained before filling the remaining slots with diverse records; outcome reports are not treated as proof of causation. Platform matching remains a weak ordering hint and other contexts retain space where available. Six workspace experience tests, the form-inspection ranking test, two model-context unit tests, formatting and web Clippy passed. No local-model comparison was run for this selection change, so any effect on model accuracy or transfer remains unmeasured.

A resource-gated qwen3.5:9b comparison on 2026-11-02 exercised the production selector with eight fictional text lessons, including conflicting `helped` and `did_not_help` reports. Both the no-memory and memory-enabled runs mapped the applicant email and a general applicant-contact field, and neither mapped the reference or manager email. The same two selectors in one fixture show no observed improvement or regression, not general reliability or an established benefit from memory. During one sampled point in the run 13.68 GiB system RAM was available; the model was subsequently unloaded. No fields were filled and no application was sent. Web Clippy passed.

The conflicting-report comparison was extended to a separate fictional multilingual form with an ambiguous updates question and a visa question carrying a misleading email autocomplete hint. In one qwen3.5:9b run per condition, both baseline and memory-enabled mapping selected only the email field and marked the other two for review. Together with the contact form this is two scenarios, not evidence of a learning benefit or broad robustness. No application was submitted; the model was unloaded and web Clippy passed.

A further read-only comparison on the previously researched public Vercel Greenhouse page used the revised selector with conflicting fictional lesson outcomes, in lesson-first order. Both passes suggested the exact fictional phone value; neither suggested a sensitive or model-flagged field. The lesson-assisted preview listed 15 review reasons and the baseline listed 19; those counts may reflect different reasons and do not establish improvement. This is one non-randomized comparison on one changing page. The resource-gated model was unloaded; no fields were filled and no application was submitted. Web Clippy passed. For pushed commit `801d9b8`, GitHub build/test, browser, PDF and tool-contract jobs passed, while the supply-chain job failed; coverage was still running at the last check.

A mixed-platform regression test found that preserving a contradictory pair could put other-platform reports ahead of same-platform reports. The selector now restores platform/time ordering after choosing members; the pair remains in the bounded set. The Windows workspace suite passed 53 tests (1 ignored, plus one ignored live integration), the form-task group passed 16 (15 ignored), and web Clippy passed. After this ordering change, a baseline-first read-only qwen3.5:9b comparison on the same public Greenhouse form again suggested the exact fictional phone in both conditions and suggested no sensitive or model-flagged fields. The model was unloaded. These two orderings on one page do not establish a general learning effect; no fields were filled and no application was sent.

### Further public application-form observations (2026-11-02)

Four newly discovered application pages were checked read-only with the isolated Crawl4AI provider and Waypoint's browser. The Zencore Lever application returned HTTP 200 (native capture SHA-256 `0476a4c012c6cb70a0d391c29bc16782135422b3d6ca5e57815b3ba3fc059521`); Waypoint saw 17 fields and a CAPTCHA. Enginow's software-development form returned HTTP 200 (native capture SHA-256 `a1f4afd33e5a02f43e0310228b182ddeee15c3a75039920e80c5139d85894dfb`); Waypoint saw 17 fields, no detected CAPTCHA, and custom-control/obscured-field review walls. NuroBlox's general application returned HTTP 200 (native capture SHA-256 `820b21bd8fb4a6189d56c3ad7d36f2a9b836ca2256c851062908ea47c4eb7e8c`); Waypoint saw five fields and the same review walls. Weblytec's careers form returned HTTP 200 (native capture SHA-256 `402925a4b35ac95b8f5f4bf90708c72e3dfb155d35b595675147d70991fa7a14`); Waypoint saw six fields, three incomplete custom-choice inventories and an unresolved button label. Counts and wall reports describe these snapshots, not full coverage or site-wide behavior. None was filled or submitted. The first crawler invocation used the system Python without the isolated provider and failed to find Crawl4AI; the isolated provider passed its doctor check and was used for all four retained captures.

### Dialog-obscured input diagnosis (2026-11-02)

On the previously captured Enginow page, a temporary read-only visibility diagnostic found that the required Full name field failed the hit test because its center hit a dialog. An independent browser view showed a cookie-choice dialog, although the two browser sessions do not prove that this exact dialog caused the other session's obstruction. Waypoint now adds a specific dialog-obstruction review reason and continues to block filling until reinspection; it does not click or dismiss the dialog. Two local browser fixtures distinguished a dialog obstruction from a generic overlay, and web Clippy and formatting passed. A subsequent live read-only inspection returned the new reason. No fields were filled and no application was submitted.

A fictional local-browser check dismissed a visible dialog using a non-submit test button and reinspected the page: the dialog warning cleared and the previously covered required field returned to the schema. This demonstrates the underlying browser rescan behavior, not automatic cookie handling or a live employer workflow. No external page was clicked or filled in this test.

### Generic click guard (2026-11-02)

The generic browser `Click` command now rejects native submit buttons, implicit-submit buttons, image-submit inputs, links, and descendants of those elements. The separate commit path remains unchanged. Seven local browser click tests passed, including a fixture with misleading “Continue” submit labels and a nested target; a separate fictional local submission fixture confirmed the explicit commit path still works. This is not a general submission-prevention guarantee: a site can attach arbitrary JavaScript or network side effects to an apparently ordinary button, and CDP does not currently provide a comprehensive write-blocking mode. No real application was submitted. Web Clippy passed.

When a form-mapping request is attempted with reviewed lessons reporting both helped and did-not-help for the same stage, control, observation and response, initial inspection and navigation/rescan now add an explicit review notice. The notice describes request preparation, not model receipt or causal evidence. A fictional unit test covered split control classes, unrelated model schemas and an actual conflicting batch; web Clippy passed. The notice has not yet been checked in a rendered live workflow.

### Offscreen custom-choice review (2026-11-02)

The closed-choice visual-review path now centers the identified control before capture and verifies the URL, form state, previously selected values and choice snapshot after scrolling. A local dynamic ARIA-combobox fixture covered an offscreen choice whose list is created on opening, visual capture, selection and retained-value verification. A separate fixture case changed the control during scroll and was rejected. At the bottom of a page where there is insufficient scroll space, some options remained outside the viewport and the inventory stayed explicitly incomplete; centering is not a guarantee that all options become visible. The 94-test local browser suite passed before the subsequent centering changes; targeted browser and web-app tests for the new behavior and web Clippy passed afterward. On the previously researched Weblytec form, a read-only location check did not scroll: the unchanged page had an unrelated button with an unresolved accessible name, which remains a manual-review blocker. No external option was opened or selected and no application was submitted.

### Reviewed choice access with an unresolved button (2026-11-02)

On the previously captured Weblytec form, the browser accessibility tree associated the unresolved reference with an upload button; one `aria-labelledby` reference was absent. A stable unresolved-button warning can now coexist with the specifically reviewed custom-choice inspection/action path. The warning is retained, the target still requires its existing visual evidence and one-use reviewed action, and ordinary fill and submission checks remain blocked. CAPTCHA, login and other unknown walls still block choice interaction. In two read-only observations after the change, one choice-location attempt stopped because the page changed during positioning, and another located the control and obtained a screenshot; neither opened or selected a choice. The 21-test non-ignored form-preview group, the offscreen-change browser fixture, formatting and web Clippy passed. No application was submitted.

A fictional offscreen-choice fixture now includes an unrelated unresolved button: the real custom-choice inventory endpoint returned `can_open=true`, while ordinary form review remained blocked; changing the choice during scroll was rejected. The choice UI check passed. Separately, a resource-gated qwen3.5:9b run exercised the custom-choice visual-review endpoint on a fictional option-list fixture and reported the label observed; it did not open, select or submit a real application. The model was unloaded, and web Clippy passed. These separate checks do not prove a complete live custom-choice action.

### Choice-label visual comparison (2026-11-02)

On the previously researched Weblytec page, a resource-gated qwen3.5:9b crop read the choice label as `Position You're Applying For`, while the browser exposed `Position You’re Applying For *`. The earlier exact comparison returned `not_observed`. Choice field-label comparison now normalizes curly apostrophes and a whitespace-separated trailing required marker; option and selected-value comparisons remain exact apart from their existing whitespace/case handling. Ambiguity checks use the same choice-label normalization and multiple visual matches remain ambiguous. In one subsequent read-only run, the model returned the same transcription, the unchanged snapshot and image passed freshness checks, and comparison returned `label_observed`. Other attempts stopped on changed-page or disappeared-field checks; a temporary diagnostic initially used an incorrect local output path and was corrected. No choice was clicked, no fields were entered and no application was submitted. Six control-vision unit tests, the custom-choice UI test and web Clippy passed; the model was unloaded. Arbitrary site scripts can still cause writes on ordinary clicks, so this does not qualify a real choice action for a no-submission guarantee.

### Browser rehearsal network qualification (2026-11-02)

A loopback-only Chromium experiment established a WebSocket, verified a baseline message, enabled `Network.emulateNetworkConditions` offline, attempted a fictional message, and restored connectivity after one second. The browser reported the send accepted; the server did not receive the message before restoration but did receive it afterward. This is one browser/version/transport observation, not a complete network-isolation evaluation. The initial exploratory test lacked a baseline and incorrectly asserted delivery while offline; that assertion failed and the revised test records observations rather than assuming the result. A temporary offline switch must therefore not be presented as a no-submission rehearsal guard. No external application was opened or submitted in this experiment. Official CDP Fetch and Network documentation was retained in `target/research/` using the isolated crawler (Fetch native SHA-256 `a3721414b422ecbec8b34748120d590165918a7b811651f8eb8f23e267d64872`; Network native SHA-256 `7703f56b96d91f99d07ed76c04057ad11fa546b820279bdab10df047964fcbe4`). A first Fetch capture with a mismatched marker was retained as a failed qualification; the subsequent capture succeeded.

A second loopback experiment attached two browser targets, established a WebSocket in the second target, and put only the first target into offline emulation. A fictional message from the second target reached the loopback server while the first remained offline. The initial second-target baseline also arrived. This confirms target-scoped emulation cannot by itself isolate the browser; other targets, workers, service workers, existing connections and race windows still need explicit treatment. No external application was involved. At this checkpoint, the serial web-app suite passed 515 tests (272 ignored), all 124 JavaScript UI checks passed, and formatting passed.

### Listing capture fingerprints (2026-11-02)

New attempt archives include an unsigned SHA-256 manifest for the original and
follow-up page capture metadata and screenshot segments. It enables later manual
comparison; the application does not yet automatically verify these hashes, and
the manifest does not authenticate the files or their origin. Existing archives
are not modified. The Windows attempt-record tests and web-app lint passed with
fictional captures.

Attempt history now compares new capture manifests with the saved metadata and
screenshot bytes, with a shared 32 MiB read budget per response and streaming
hashing. It reports matches, differences, unavailable or legacy manifests, and
budget exhaustion. The hashes remain unsigned and do not establish authenticity;
older captures have no manifest. A Windows archive test covered a match and
altered screenshot, and focused tests covered missing files and the read limit.

### Split phone fields (2026-11-02)

In one fictional local-model run, qwen3.5:9b proposed the stored international
phone number for a phone field while leaving separate calling-code and extension
fields for review. A recognized calling-code label or `tel-country-code` hint now
adds a warning when the proposed profile phone starts with `+`: the user should
check whether the form expects a national number. This heuristic does not cover
all languages or layouts, split numbers, or prove the resulting format is valid.
The same local-model scenario passed after the change; the model was unloaded.
Focused suggestion tests and web Clippy passed. No application was submitted.

### Explicit split-phone profile values (2026-11-02)

The profile can now store an explicitly supplied national phone number, calling
code and extension alongside the complete phone number. The mapper recognizes
matching labels and telephone autocomplete hints, rejects conflicting hinted
components, and never derives a component from another value. The normal
review gates still apply. A Windows restart test confirmed the new profile
values persist; fictional deterministic mapping tests and web Clippy passed.

In a resource-gated qwen3.5:9b test with all components supplied and matching
hints, the model-assisted path suggested the calling code and extension but
its national-number suggestion was rejected as invalid; the opt-in test
therefore fails and the number remains for manual review. A prompt experiment
also caused the model to withhold the full number in the incomplete-profile
case, so it was removed. This is not a validated end-to-end solution for split
phone forms, and no third-party form was filled or submitted. The model was
unloaded after the test.

The split-phone local-model diagnostic now records the fictional raw mapping.
In repeated runs the model chose `phone` for a `tel-national` field even with
`phone_national` available; the validator withheld it. A targeted prompt, a
single-field retry, and per-field eligible-key hints did not correct this in
these trials and were removed. The ignored local-model test still fails for that
expected mapping. Invalid suggestions with a recognized autocomplete component
now show the component in their review message; a hint does not prove whose
information the field requests. Focused suggestion tests and web Clippy passed;
the model was unloaded. No application was submitted.

A follow-up fixed a regression introduced by the detailed invalid-suggestion
message: after a user entered a replacement, the old review-refresh logic did
not recognize the expanded warning and left it active. The message generator
and refresh logic now share the same warning format; duplicate labels remain
flagged until each matching field has an answer. The web-app suite passed 536
tests (286 ignored) with two test threads, and web Clippy passed. This does not
resolve the local model's national-phone mapping failure described above.

### Selecting a saved value during form review (2026-11-02)

An unresolved, non-sensitive text or email field can now use an explicitly
selected saved contact value, including split phone components. The picker shows
field names, not values, until the user chooses one; the inserted answer remains
editable and still requires the normal reviewed fill action. Sensitive and
choice controls do not expose this picker. A simulated UI test checked explicit
selection, the allowed-field list and sensitive-field exclusion; it does not
establish behavior on a live employer form.

Reordering the profile keys and renaming the full-phone key in the local-model
prompt did not correct the fictional national-phone mapping in individual
resource-gated trials, so those experiments were removed. The ignored mapping
test remains failing for the national number. No application was submitted.

A rendered fictional localhost fixture using the picker and application styles
confirmed that selecting a saved national number places it in the editable
answer field. This was a standalone fixture, not the full application or an
employer site. During this check, selecting a saved profile value was found to
leave a previous cover-letter provenance link attached to the overwritten text.
The picker now clears answer and cover-letter draft links and invalidates an
in-flight letter-link update before copying the value. Focused picker and draft
provenance UI tests passed. No application was submitted.

Saved contact values can now also replace an existing, non-sensitive text
suggestion during form review; the explicit choice updates the editable answer
and selects it for the later reviewed fill. It does not act on the employer
page. A simulated UI check covered selection and the resulting input event.
The complete JavaScript test run exposed a stale isolated form-test harness
that lacked the picker dependency; after updating the harness, all 128 checks
passed. The expanded flow has not been exercised on a real employer form.

### Split-phone model comparison availability (2026-11-02)

The opt-in fictional split-phone test can now select an installed model while
using the same resource-gated startup and owned-model cleanup. Attempts to run
`gemma4:12b-it-q4_K_M` and `gemma4:12b-it-qat` were refused before loading:
the gate observed 10,121 MiB free versus 11,229 MiB required, and 10,118 MiB
free versus 10,232 MiB required, respectively. These are point-in-time memory
estimates; neither model was evaluated. A separate Qwen multi-role listing test
passed through the refactored helper (one excerpt retained, two withheld), and
Ollama reported no resident model afterward. This does not resolve the split
phone mapping issue or establish comparative model quality. Web Clippy passed.

### Search-link tracking variants (2026-11-02)

A wider-web batch now compares URLs after removing selected common tracking
parameters, while keeping the first original URL and retaining other query
parameters and fragments in the comparison key. Coverage reports how many later
links were omitted as equivalent. This is a heuristic: a site that gives one of
those parameters substantive meaning could have a distinct link suppressed.
It does not establish that different domains advertise different jobs or prevent
all duplicate applications. Fictional batch and coverage tests passed, as did
web Clippy; no live provider or application was contacted in this check.

The tracking-variant search heuristic was revised after recognizing that a site
may use a tracking-looking parameter to identify a different role. New batches
keep the original variants as separately selectable candidates and label later
variants for comparison; exact repeated URLs remain deduplicated. These variants
count toward the existing 600-link cap. Previously completed batches cannot
recover links omitted by the earlier implementation; their coverage notice now
identifies that earlier omission. Fictional batch and UI checks and web Clippy
passed. No live provider or application was contacted in this check.

Related search variants now retain the first matching URL and offer a direct
comparison link on the later candidate. A fictional two-page loopback provider
confirmed that both variants and the comparison reference remain available in
completed batch history; simulated UI checks covered the link and rejected a
non-HTTPS reference. This does not prove that the listings are duplicates or
restore links omitted by older batches. Web Clippy passed; no live provider or
application was contacted.

### Employer instructions in form review (2026-11-02)

A read-only inspection of a public Lever application found an instruction to
answer a section without AI assistance. The browser exposed 19 fields; four
text areas were already marked for manual review, but the instruction was not
reliably present in each field label. Form inspection now scans up to 32,000
bytes of page text for a small set of explicit no-AI phrases and adds an
advisory to review the original instruction and its scope. Truncated or failed
text checks are reported. This is a limited phrase heuristic over DOM text,
which may include hidden content; it is neither a complete policy classifier
nor an automatic determination of which questions are affected.

The production inspection worker reported the advisory on that page in one
read-only run; no fields were filled and no application was sent. A preceding
robots-checked Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 capture returned HTTP 200
(native SHA-256 `3615ce867a5cd0a2a712f211ca7d9ac24ccc67011344622ad876c7cc54ec6ceb`;
markdown SHA-256 `a9f4aa91f433e06ed52b0ef7bd291faaa806ca24fa354f160b4f1b5fd74f84cf`)
for `https://jobs.lever.co/Nomagic/fafe0fa1-f6b8-4004-a869-cbe587325067/apply`.
No local model was loaded. Focused form-task tests and web Clippy passed.

### Context from headed containers (2026-11-02)

A public Lever form placed its section instruction in a heading inside an
ordinary container, so question labels lacked that context. The browser now
includes a single direct, visibly rendered heading from such a container as a
`Possible section` in descendant field labels. This is an inference, not proof
of semantic grouping; broad container headings can also appear and should be
reviewed. Unverifiable headings remain review warnings, and changes to captured
context change the form schema identity.

A fictional local-browser fixture checked two questions, a hidden heading and a
changed heading. On the previously captured public form, four text-area labels
then included the instruction. In one read-only, resource-gated qwen3.5:9b run,
the browser already marked the instruction-bearing controls for review; none
was suggested, and the fictional phone value was suggested. This does not show
that the model independently recognized the instruction or establish general
accuracy. A browser test run before the final `Possible section` labeling passed
103 cases and failed two public probes because their URL environment variables
were absent; the affected headed-container and existing semantic-heading cases
passed after the labeling change. Browser and web Clippy passed. The model was
unloaded; no fields were filled and no application was submitted.

The section-context traversal now shares a 50,000-node budget across group
subtrees and reports incomplete context when that budget is exhausted. Plain
div headings are inferred only when the container has no ARIA role or has a
group/region role; unrelated roles are excluded. A fictional browser fixture
covered the role exclusion and heading change, and a unit test checked shared
budget exhaustion. The previously reviewed public form still exposed its
instruction-bearing labels in a read-only check. Browser library tests and
Clippy passed. This bounds the repeated group scan, not the browser's whole DOM
retrieval or total memory use; no application was submitted.

A fictional browser fixture with 35 nested headed containers and 100 inert
children per level exercised the shared section-context budget. The browser
returned the email field and an explicit incomplete-context warning in one run.
Script, style, template and noscript subtrees are now skipped during the group
scan. Earlier fixture variants with 80 and 330 nested containers failed during
DOM response decoding (the Rust JSON recursion limit and Chromium's protocol
stack limit, respectively); those depths are not supported by this check. The
focused browser fixture and browser Clippy passed. No application was submitted.

### Moderately deeper form responses (2026-11-02)

DOM replies that hit the default JSON recursion limit now receive one fallback
parse on an 8 MiB stack after a string-aware 224-level nesting check; protocol
messages remain capped at 16 MiB. Non-DOM replies retain the standard decoder.
If a field's visibility cannot be established, the browser leaves that field
out and reports manual review rather than discarding other inspected fields.
In a fictional 80-container fixture, the shallower field was retained and the
deeper field was reported unavailable. This does not overcome Chromium's own
protocol stack limit observed at 330 containers, prove total memory bounds, or
establish live-site behavior. Browser unit tests, focused browser checks and
Clippy passed; no application was submitted.

The required workspace checks were also run after enabling serde_json's
`unbounded_depth` feature. `Cargo.lock` did not change. `cargo audit` reported
three zip advisories and five denied warnings in the existing lockfile;
`cargo deny check licenses bans sources` reported license failures while bans
and sources passed. These workspace gates were not repaired in this change.

### Read-only Workable form observation (2026-11-02)

The ignored live application-check diagnostic now accepts the reviewed
`apply.workable.com` host and checks the source registry before opening it.
Two read-only runs against one public application page passed. The first direct
inspection found eight fields, while the separate application check found none;
the second found seven fields and reported a possible CAPTCHA, obscured fields,
required custom controls and a hidden required control. The second application
check also reported obstacles and required manual review. This variation is not
enough to establish its cause or reliable Workable support. No candidate details
were entered and no application was submitted. This observation does not verify
filling, visual verification or multi-step completion.

### Fields disappearing during initial loading (2026-11-02)

When an initially empty page is inspected again during its bounded loading
window, fields that disappear between snapshots now create a persistent manual
review reason. The latest snapshot remains authoritative for fill targets; an
earlier field is not reused. If no fields remain, the application check reports
the disappearance rather than stating simply that none were detected. A unit
test covers field growth, loss and recovery, and a fictional local browser
fixture reproduces loss during loading. This does not establish the cause of the
varying Workable observations or detect changes outside the loading window.

### Initially partial forms (2026-11-02)

The browser now takes a second snapshot after a short 350 ms wait even when
the first snapshot contains fields. If the schema changes, it continues within
the existing bounded loading loop and preserves field-loss review reasons.
This adds at least one DOM inspection to these navigations and does not detect
fields that appear after the snapshot is judged stable. A fictional local form
that adds a field shortly after load, the staged-loading fixture and the
disappearing-field fixture passed. One subsequent read-only Workable diagnostic
still produced inconsistent separate observations: the first found no fields,
while the application check reported possible CAPTCHA, required custom and
hidden-control obstacles. It required manual review and did not fill or submit.

### Consistent application-link review (2026-11-02)

The form-inspection worker now applies the same bounded, label-aware candidate
filter as the read-only application checker before following a likely link.
If a different visible link to the same destination has a final-action label,
or the link inventory cannot be read, navigation is left for manual review.
The worker also checks that the page URL has not changed during the inventory.
Fictional driver tests cover a conflicting submit label and an ordinary link.
This is a conservative filter, not proof that arbitrary links cannot submit.

### Blank requisition identifiers (2026-11-02)

Discovery identity keys now treat whitespace-only requisition identifiers as
absent and use the listing URL instead. This avoids assigning different listings
from the same employer the same blank-requisition key. Nonblank identifiers keep
their original bytes to avoid silently changing existing identities. Search unit
tests cover empty, ASCII-whitespace and nonbreaking-space identifiers and
distinct listing URLs. Existing records are not repaired or split by this change.

### Unchanged listings refresh their observation time (2026-11-02)

When a source returns an unchanged listing, discovery now advances its saved
last-seen time without changing the original first-seen time or application
state. Older or nonpositive timestamps cannot move the last-seen value backward,
including on changed listings. Fictional multi-source rediscovery and
shortlisted-job tests passed. Last seen means the listing was observed in the
source response; it does not establish that an application page is open or that
the role remains available.

### Source-local identifier collisions (2026-11-02)

When two sources produce the same employer namespace and requisition key,
discovery now keeps the second source in a separate, stable source-scoped
record instead of overwriting the first. Subsequent observations reuse that
record; possible duplicates still go through the existing review process. A
fictional Lever/Ashby collision test covers rediscovery and preservation of a
shortlisted state. Existing records already overwritten by an earlier collision
are not reconstructed. This does not establish that every cross-source duplicate
will be detected.

### Resource-gated multi-step model check (2026-11-02)

A new ignored test runs the existing fictional two-step form exercise through
the app's model resource gate and unloads a model it started. In one run,
qwen3.5:9b proposed and filled a given name and email on separate steps, left
the sensitive work-authorization field empty, and visually transcribed the two
filled values as matching. No application was submitted. Ollama reported a
5.5 GB, fully GPU-resident model during the run; sampled free system memory
was about 14.1 GiB during the run and 19.7 GiB after unloading. These are
snapshots, not peak measurements. One fictional run does not establish
reliability on real multi-step applications or general model accuracy.

The same gated wizard test can select an installed model through
`WAYPOINT_VISION_BENCHMARK_MODEL`. A qwen3.5:9b-q8_0 attempt did not run:
the startup gate reported 20,159 MiB available and required at least
20,206 MiB. The model was not started and no comparison result was obtained.
The gate was not relaxed; available memory is a changing snapshot.

### Multilingual contact context (2026-11-02)

The resource-gated multilingual contact test now obtains its six field labels
from a fictional page in a local browser rather than constructing the schema
directly. In one qwen3.5:9b run, the model suggested the candidate email for
the German, French and Spanish applicant fields and marked all three reference
email fields for review. The test did not fill fields or submit. The model was
unloaded after the test; free system memory sampled afterward was about 19.6 GiB.
This single mapping check does not establish visual verification or performance
on real applications.

The same fictional multilingual browser test was subsequently extended through
reviewed prefill and a local visual pass. In two qwen3.5:9b runs, it filled all
three applicant fields, left the three reference fields empty, and visually
transcribed the applicant values correctly. The second run also asserted that
the visual observations reported each reference field empty. DOM checks agreed;
no application was submitted. These are two runs of one fixture, not a general
accuracy measurement.

### Read-only local-model inspection of a public form (2026-11-02)

A generic resource-gated diagnostic can now inspect a policy-permitted public
form with a fictional profile without clicking, typing or submitting. Two runs
against a Greenhouse-hosted Synack form observed ten fields and four suggested
contact mappings. On the second run the proposed first name, last name, email
and phone matched the fictional profile. The inspector also reported a possible
CAPTCHA, an uninspected embedded frame, custom/hidden controls and required
fields needing review. No real-site filling or visual verification was tested.
A separate search result redirected to its employer's board with a closed-job
message, illustrating why search snippets alone cannot establish freshness.
The local model was unloaded; these observations are not a general success rate.

### Same-site listing redirects (2026-11-02)

Read-only application checks now report when the initial listing redirects to
a different path on the same origin, without disclosing the destination query.
This is an advisory to verify the destination, not a closed-job determination.
On one public Greenhouse listing independently observed to show a closed-job
message, the browser check reported the path change and required manual review.
A fictional URL test covers unchanged paths, external origins and query privacy;
the focused test and web Clippy passed. No application was submitted.
The form-review worker also stops automatic link-following after this kind of
redirect and carries the same notice into review; a fictional driver test
checks that it stays on the redirected page without exposing the query.

### Search index-date provenance (2026-11-02)

When repeated search results report different index dates, the result now marks
its date attribution as limited rather than silently presenting the retained
date as the only report. The flag carries through multi-page result selection
and saved search context. Fictional parser and interface tests passed. Only one
index date is currently retained; this change does not establish listing freshness.

The result parser and selection flow were subsequently extended to retain up
to four additional distinct index dates. They are displayed and preserved in
saved search context; further dates set the existing limited-attribution flag.
Older saved contexts without the new field remain readable. Fictional parser,
selection, persistence and interface tests passed, as did web Clippy. Provider
index dates remain unverified and do not establish that a listing is open.

### Suspected non-vacancy results (2026-11-02)

A model-classified possible non-vacancy now returns a review-only inspection
result instead of automatically creating a job. The search result remains
available for manual entry because the model can misclassify a genuine role.
Batch inspection does not check an application page for that result, and the UI
does not present its mentioned role titles as vacancies. A fictional endpoint
test verifies no job is created and that the result can be retrieved again;
single and batch UI tests passed. In a fresh isolated Windows workspace, a real
web search returned an article; qwen3.5:9b flagged it, the UI kept zero jobs,
and manual entry remained available. No real application was filled or sent.
This does not prevent false negatives when the classifier says uncertain or
vacancy, and older mistakenly saved records are not removed.

For a suspected non-vacancy, extraction now stops after classification instead
of asking the model to rank other roles or audit a purported employer. The
single-result interface offers an explicit way to place the unverified extracted
title and employer into the manual-entry form for correction; it does not save
them automatically. Fictional tests verify that no further model calls occur
and that the recovery action uses the original result URL. The revised shortcut
has not yet been measured on a live page, and model classifications can be wrong.

### Uncertain page classification (2026-11-02)

Local-model extractions classified as uncertain now also remain review-only
rather than being automatically saved as vacancies. Structured job results
are unchanged. Users can inspect the original and enter details manually, so
a genuine vacancy the model cannot classify requires extra review. In one
resource-gated qwen3.5:9b comparison of two public text captures, a career
article was classified uncertain and a Greenhouse application page was
classified vacancy. The test was initially written to expect a stronger
non-vacancy result and failed; after adopting review-only handling for uncertain
results, it passed with those same classifications. Three fictional vacancies
were also classified as vacancies in a separate run. These small samples do
not establish general accuracy. The model was unloaded and no application sent.

### Bounded fallback for long page classification (2026-11-02)

When the first local classification of a page longer than 6,000 bytes is
uncertain, Waypoint makes one additional classification using bounded excerpts
from the beginning, middle and end. Only a non-vacancy result changes the
review reason; a sampled vacancy does not turn an uncertain page into an
automatically saved job. The interface warns when the negative judgment used
excerpts, since omitted text can change the meaning. On one public article and
one public application-page capture, qwen3.5:9b returned non-vacancy and vacancy
respectively after this change; earlier the article returned uncertain. Fictional
tests cover the no-upgrade rule and warning propagation. These examples do not
establish general accuracy or prove why the longer input was uncertain.

### Real résumé fit diagnostic and bounded sampling (2026-11-02)

A resource-gated qwen3.5:9b diagnostic compared 88 automatically segmented,
unverified entries from one public résumé with one public vacancy. The corrected
diagnostic uses the same bounded fact pool as the application; 50 entries entered
the pool, all were examined, and 18 were selected. The assessment reported three
criteria, including one unknown, with incomplete requirement coverage and a
67–100 score range. The listing-only recovery was needed, so this result is not
evidence of comprehensive or accurate matching. One sample cannot establish
general accuracy. Source and candidate text and detailed results remain outside
the repository; no application was sent. The model was unloaded afterwards.

The evenly spaced pool now includes the last entry as well as the first, within
the existing limit. A focused fictional test checks the endpoints. The scoring
version was advanced so previously cached assessments are not mistaken for
results generated with the changed pool.

### Formatted listing excerpts (2026-11-02)

The listing-only recovery now recognizes paired Markdown bold delimiters while
preserving original source bytes and rejecting ambiguous matches. In a repeat of
the same private-input diagnostic, three excerpts previously rejected because of
formatting were recovered and six criteria survived. The separate candidate
evidence search was unavailable, however, so all six remained unknown and the
score range was 0–100. This is not a successful fit judgment; improving bounded
evidence comparison remains necessary. The model was unloaded afterwards.

### Bounded evidence comparison (2026-11-02)

Oversized comparisons now split requirements and selected facts into at most
12 bounded model requests. Every selected requirement is compared against every
selected fact, and invalid responses, cross-batch references, conflicting
judgments, and incomplete batches cannot silently establish evidence. The
separate qualification check remains necessary before a proposal affects the
score. On the same single public résumé and vacancy diagnostic, two batches
completed, one of six criteria was supported, and five remained unknown; the
score range was 25–100 and requirement coverage still needed review. This is one
unverified input pair, not a general accuracy measurement. The model was
unloaded and no application was sent.

### Grouped qualification checks (2026-11-02)

One diagnostic exposed a grouped criterion marked supported even though its
cited fact did not name several listed elements. Stronger instructions alone
did not correct that result. A separate local-model check now requests individual
source-backed components; invalid, incomplete or unsupported checks leave the
criterion unknown. This remains a heuristic and does not prove that the model
identified every component or interpreted the source correctly.

On the same real-input diagnostic, five grouped judgments were checked; three
produced structurally valid results and all five were left unknown. The score
range was 0–100. This is a material usability limitation of this model and check,
not a successful fit assessment. The model was unloaded and no application was
sent.

The grouped check was then evaluated separately with three fictional cases:
a fully supported conjunction, a partially supported conjunction, and an
explicit alternative. The local model returned the expected three statuses in
this small test. Component quotes can now recover paired Markdown formatting
from the source; overlapping component quotes are rejected. In a separate
repeat using five grouped judgments from the earlier real-input assessment,
three responses were structurally usable and all five judgments remained
unknown. The detailed trace remains outside the repository. These examples do
not establish general accuracy, and the real-input result remains inconclusive.

### Workable application obstacle explanation (2026-11-02)

A read-only check of one public Workable application observed four fields and
reported a possible verification challenge, required custom and hidden controls,
and a dialog and potentially obscured fields. The summary previously omitted
the latter two signals. It now explains them and advises checking the visible
page and resolving any blocking overlay before inspecting again. The change uses
recognized browser signals and does not echo question text. A repeat read-only
check displayed the new explanation; fictional tests cover the summary. This
does not establish that the application can be filled or submitted, nor that
the challenge and hidden-control signals are true positives. No candidate data
was entered and no application was submitted. Two other search results were
stale: one led to a closed-job page and one returned HTTP 404.

### Guarded cookie-control diagnosis (2026-11-02)

A read-only screenshot of that form showed a cookie-consent dialog. An attempted
guarded click on its visually identified decline button was refused because the
button had no explicit type; the dialog remained visible and no applicant data
was entered or submitted. A local fixture confirms that implicit and explicit
submit controls remain blocked. The browser now reports that such a target
could submit or navigate instead of incorrectly calling it hidden or covered.
The screenshot diagnostic is opt-in and writes only to a new user-selected
local file. That guarded attempt did not dismiss the dialog or resume the form.

### Visible-session reinspection on a public application (2026-11-02)

In a separate, visible isolated browser session on the same public Workable
form, the operator used the visible cookie-consent control to decline optional
cookies. Reinspection of that same browser session found seven fields instead of
four, and the dialog-obstruction warning cleared; four other review reasons
remained. No applicant information was entered and no application was submitted.
This verifies the browser driver's same-session rescan in one case; it does not
verify automatic dialog handling, filling, or completion. An ignored diagnostic retains the visible session briefly for an
operator to perform a reviewed action and then requests reinspection.

### Application-worker rescan on a public form (2026-11-02)

An ignored live diagnostic ran the application inspection worker in a visible
isolated browser, then invoked its normal rescan worker with the same retained
session after the operator declined optional cookies. The preview increased
from four observed fields to seven; the dialog warning cleared, while eleven
review reasons remained. The profile was empty, no model was loaded, and no
applicant data was entered or submitted. This covers the workers, not the full
rendered web interface or automatic dialog handling, and does not establish that
the form can be filled or completed.

With a fictional profile and resource-gated qwen3.5:9b, the same worker rescan
was repeated twice on this form after the operator declined optional cookies.
Both runs observed seven fields and returned four suggestions matching the
fictional first name, last name, email and phone; nine review reasons remained.
The second run asserted those mappings. This checks one form and profile, not
general mapping accuracy, actual filling, visual verification or submission.
No applicant data was entered and the model was unloaded afterward.

### Phone country-picker review (2026-11-02)

The live form's phone field has a separate country-picker button. The previous
mapping test confirmed only that the proposed text matched the fictional
profile; it did not establish that the site's selected country and number would
combine correctly. When a proposed international number accompanies a recognized
phone-country control, Waypoint now withholds that mapping and requires manual
handling. An incomplete control inventory also requires review. The check runs
on initial inspection, rescan and immediately before reviewed filling; it does
not infer a country or number format. Its English-label and autocomplete cues
do not cover every language or widget.

On one repeat of the same public form with the fictional profile and local
model, three name/email suggestions remained and the phone field was marked for
manual review. No field was filled, no application was sent, and the model was
unloaded. Focused fictional tests cover the recognized control, unrelated
controls and incomplete inspection; this does not prove detection on all forms.

The same review now applies to national-format phone suggestions, since their
meaning also depends on the selected country. The field notice uses neutral
review language rather than attributing this rule to an AI judgment. A
fictional test covers the national-number case; this extension has not been
tested by filling a live form.

### Multi-passage requirement audit (2026-11-02)

The requirement audit now scans up to six overlapping, bounded passages and
reports omitted sections or interrupted scans. A later invalid or unavailable
pass discards earlier additions. Evidence search can compare all twelve rubric
criteria in batches of at most six. In the same single real-input diagnostic,
two audit passages covered the captured listing and added two possible criteria;
all eight criteria then entered evidence search. Three were supported after
independent checking and five remained unknown. The resulting 36–100 range and
listing-only recovery still require review. This is not a general accuracy
measurement; the model was unloaded and no application was sent.

Preference comparison now samples the beginning, middle and end of long
listings instead of only the first 3,000 bytes. Quotes must occur wholly in one
supplied passage; omitted sections remain explicitly incomplete, and existing
assessments become stale under the updated input version. A fictional test
recovered a late remote-work statement and rejected a gap marker as evidence.
The fit test group passed (70 passed, 39 ignored); no local model or real job
was tested in this change, and the sample still cannot establish full coverage
or the correctness of a model's judgment.
The sampler also excludes partial omission markers when a sample starts or
ends inside one, and limits the number of separate passages to 16. A fictional
boundary test covers both cases; preference tests passed (12 passed, 5 ignored).
The input version changed again, so earlier assessments need refreshing. No
model was loaded for this check.

### Partial-edge edits across PDF text objects (2026-11-02)

The guarded PDF editor can now attempt a replacement that begins inside the
first text object and ends inside the last of a consecutive group, preserving
the untouched prefix and suffix. It first tries the existing encoding-preserving
stream editor and falls back to PDFium; either path must pass the existing
whole-document text, glyph, bounds and rendered-difference checks. A fictional
mixed-color example preserved surrounding text and text-object colors and was
rendered for visual inspection. Expanding replacements and other complex layouts
remain restricted.

Ten candidate boundaries from one public ten-page résumé were tried locally.
All were refused by existing text or visual checks, so this does not establish
that the new path works on that résumé or on arbitrary PDFs. The source and any
derived material remain outside the repository; no application was sent.

Follow-up diagnostics found that three shortening failures inserted an inferred
space into extracted text. The stream path now also tries the selected span when
the complete text object cannot be matched, while retaining the same final
checks. A fictional test exercised that fallback and preserved text and colors.
On the same public résumé, ten shortening attempts and three equal-width
substitutions still failed the existing text, bounds or visual checks. No real
edit was accepted; these results do not establish a general success rate.

Further investigation of the first page found that concatenating decodable
text-show operations did not reproduce every PDFium text selection: of 696
unique six-character selections without line breaks, 203 occurred exactly once
in that concatenation and 493 did not occur. Five unique six-character samples
crossing exactly two PDFium text objects were all absent or ambiguous in the
concatenation. This is a diagnostic on one page, not a benchmark. Matching
across operators alone therefore cannot safely replace the existing checks;
the mapping between rendered characters and source operators needs further
investigation.

In a separate first-page probe, three unique-on-page text objects could be
matched to a stream operation; one shortening passed the complete document,
glyph, bounds and visual checks, while two were refused because their text
also occurred elsewhere in the document. The output was kept in memory. A
subsequent change lets the stream editor reuse a glyph byte from the selected
passage when the font maps multiple bytes to the same character, provided the
passage itself uses only one such byte. Fictional tests cover reuse and refusal
when the source provides no unique byte. On ten first-page objects of this
résumé, the change did not alter which stream edits matched (three before and
after); it is not a demonstrated fix for the remaining résumé failures.

### Custom LibreOffice location (2026-11-02)

Local tools settings can now save an existing absolute `soffice.com` path for
nonstandard LibreOffice installations, or clear it to restore automatic
detection. The existing `WAYPOINT_SOFFICE` environment override takes precedence.
The path must name an existing file when saved and used; the setting does not
install or execute the converter or prove it works. A temporary-file test covers
validation, persistence, missing files and clearing; a mocked UI test covers
the settings request. A separate isolated Windows app probe selected a
nonstandard path linked to the existing LibreOffice installation, uploaded a
fictional text file and converted it to PDF; extracted text matched. Linking
only the program directory failed, while linking the complete installation
worked. This does not verify a separately installed copy or clean-host setup.
The local probe requires an existing build, LibreOffice and PDF Python runtime;
it restores its environment and removes only its own temporary workspace.
The settings panel now reloads the saved path and reports when that file is
missing, while leaving an in-progress edit untouched. A mocked UI test covers
the edit race and a local app probe checked reporting before and after the
linked converter path was removed. The path check does not execute LibreOffice.
An isolated running-app check rendered this setup at 1,200 and 390 pixels,
rejected an invalid path and saved a cleared setting at both sizes. The check
found no page exceptions or horizontal overflow. Feedback and adjacent buttons
were given additional spacing after visual review. The Browser plugin was not
available; the check used local Playwright Core and Edge. This does not verify
screen-reader behavior or a clean-host setup.

### Packaged PDF runtime check (2026-11-02)

The package builder already supplies a pinned Python NuGet runtime and PDF
libraries. A locally built package using cached, hash-checked inputs was
installed into a new test directory without a shortcut, LibreOffice or Ollama.
With an isolated workspace, no Python override and a system-only PATH, the
installed app selected its bundled Python, passed the PDF dependency probe and
extracted text from a fictional PDF. The reusable local probe is
`apps/web_app/tests/packaged-pdf-live.ps1`. Its first run falsely reported the
runtime missing because it compared Windows paths with different separators;
normalizing both paths fixed the test, and the repeat passed. This is not a
clean-host or full-package installation test, and model and conversion setup
were excluded. Python's [Windows documentation](https://docs.python.org/3.13/using/windows.html#the-embeddable-package)
also notes that the embeddable distribution omits pip and needs installer-managed
dependencies; the existing NuGet-based package path was retained. The isolated
Crawl4AI capture had HTTP 200 and SHA-256
`08f34e95fa2db1ebc22c70c890efe610b6b6f751a356245747d3e600b7c21f82` for
extracted Markdown (provider `0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2`).

### Email contacts on application pages (2026-11-02)

When an application check follows an eligible application link, it now also
collects bounded email-contact evidence from the destination page if that page
has no detected walls. The result and unsent-draft controls distinguish listing
contacts from application-page contacts; neither is treated as a confirmed
recipient. Each page retains the existing eight-contact limit. A fictional
browser fixture checked destination-page extraction, the existing local worker
test checked listing contacts, and a mocked UI checked the page label and the
two-page contact limit. Automatic link following and destination collection
were not tested together against a live employer site; no email was sent.

Contact actions now also appear for a checked board-provided application URL
when both its listing URL and application URL still match the current job.
Previously the interface only allowed a direct listing URL, hiding those
contacts. A mocked UI test covers the current route and rejects changed or
missing route bindings; this does not verify recipient suitability.
When the check starts directly at a board-provided application URL, its
contacts are labeled as application-page contacts rather than listing
contacts. A local browser worker test covers both starting routes and result
replay; the board binding in that fixture is fictional.

### Post-fill form changes (2026-11-02)

After filling reviewed answers and checking their retained values, Waypoint now
inspects the current form again. A changed origin, field inventory, step
structure or review-relevant wall makes the fill incomplete and flags the job
for manual review through the existing failure path. Answers already entered
are not rolled back. A fictional driver test exercises a required field that
appears after filling; a separate local-browser fixture confirms an unchanged
form still fills without submission. This is a point-in-time inspection, not a
guarantee against later asynchronous page changes, and has not been tested on
a live employer form.

A second local-browser fixture now reveals a required question when the first
answer is entered. The real browser fill path detected the changed form,
reported the fill as incomplete and retained the already-entered value; it did
not submit. This verifies the browser behavior on that fixture, not the full
web endpoint or arbitrary employer pages.

When that post-fill check detects a changed form, the review panel now offers
to prepare a lesson for explicit review. It starts with an unknown outcome and
platform, asks the user to check the control type and any existing note, and
does not save or share the note automatically. A fictional UI test covers the
handoff; an API test persists the new observation category, and a fake model
test confirms that a reviewed category can enter bounded advisory context.
This does not establish that the local model learns from or acts correctly on
the lesson.

### Redirected listing identity (2026-11-02)

When an inspected listing redirects to a different path on the same site, the
extracted result now starts with a warning that it may describe a different
position. It asks the user to verify the original listing and destination;
it does not claim the job is closed or expose redirect query parameters. A
read-only browser check confirmed the warning on one previously observed public
job URL that redirected to its employer's careers page. A fictional test covers
warning order and query privacy. The check does not prove that every redirect
points to a different role, and no listing was saved or application sent.

In a subsequent resource-gated worker run, the model extracted a title that
did not match the title previously observed in the search result. The status
endpoint would previously have saved that extraction automatically. Same-site
path redirects are now review-only: the endpoint returns the extracted details
for inspection without creating a job record. A repeat against that public URL
confirmed zero saved jobs; a fictional endpoint test also checks replay. This
conservative rule can require review for legitimate canonical redirects and
does not by itself prove that a job is closed. The model was unloaded.

Redirect review now exposes the captured destination only after validating it
as a same-origin HTTPS path, so a user can inspect it separately. Manual entry
starts with the original URL but leaves title and employer empty rather than
copying potentially unrelated extracted details. A live status check returned
the destination without saving a job; focused backend and interface tests cover
cross-origin rejection and blank manual entry. This remains a manual review,
including for legitimate canonical redirects.

### Choice advice evaluation accounting (2026-11-02)

The fictional radio-advice evaluation now runs all seven cases and reports
incorrect suggestions, unexpected abstentions and inference errors separately;
two cases use the deterministic exact-match path and five exercise the model.
Previously inference errors could be silently ignored. The non-live advice tests
pass, but this change has not been qualified with the live model. A separate
live custom-choice attempt stopped before loading a model because the resource
gate observed 9,572 MiB GPU memory free against its 9,605 MiB minimum. The
previous natural-language custom-choice abstention therefore remains unresolved.

### Local choice verification and GPU headroom (2026-11-02)

The startup estimate retains a full model-sized system allocation plus 10 GiB
of available RAM, and the GPU estimate retains 20% model-size overhead plus
1.875 GiB fixed headroom (128 MiB less than before). These are snapshots, not
reservations, and are not proof that other applications cannot increase usage.
A boundary test checks the revised GPU threshold.

With qwen3.5:9b, the fictional preference example initially abstained in the
consistency review. After clarifying that an explicit preference can support a
selection, a ten-case choice evaluation passed 10/10: two deterministic exact
matches and eight model cases, including negation and uncertainty. A separate
fictional browser workflow passed with live vision and choice advice. The model
was unloaded after each run. These small, development-set observations are not
a held-out reliability or resource-peak measurement; broader evaluation remains
necessary. One intervening model start was refused by the unchanged RAM gate.

### Adversarial choice evaluation (2026-11-02)

A separate nine-case fictional choice evaluation found that qwen3.5:9b
selected an option when the supplied answer contained only an instruction to
the assistant, without a candidate preference. The other eight cases matched
the expected outcome, including uncertainty, negation, and an explicit
preference followed by a conflicting instruction. A prompt-only refinement
did not fix the failure and was discarded. This is a known model limitation,
not evidence of robust prompt-injection resistance; the resource-gated test
remains ignored by default and fails until that case is resolved. An initial
eight-case run incorrectly expected abstention despite an explicit preference;
the expected result was corrected and a separate instruction-only case added.

The custom-choice and radio review screens now ask for the candidate's fact or
preference and explain that visual recognition does not verify the suggested
answer. Fictional interface tests cover both messages. This guidance does not
prevent model misinterpretation and does not resolve the adversarial failure.

### Screenshot capture bounds (2026-11-02)

Attempt screenshot storage rejects oversized base64 input before decoding it,
avoiding a second large allocation. A focused test checks rejection without
creating a file and acceptance at the 6 MiB decoded boundary. This limits the
archive decoder; it does not measure or bound memory used by the browser or
other stages before the capture reaches it.

### Immediate ARIA validation after text entry (2026-11-02)

Text filling now checks the site's ARIA invalid state before reporting the
individual operation successful, in addition to the existing later retention
checks. A local browser fixture passed for both valid input and a field that
sets aria-invalid during typing; the existing ARIA verification fixture also
passed. This immediate check cannot detect validation the site performs later.

Immediate ARIA validation now also covers checkbox and date fills and native
single-select, multi-select and radio changes; the latter recheck the live
selection before returning. A local fixture passed for five controls that set
an invalid state on change, and four existing local-browser selection/date
tests passed. A later asynchronous validation change still needs a later check.

Checkbox fills also check native browser validity before reporting success. A
fictional browser fixture confirms that an unchecked required checkbox is
reported invalid and that checking it succeeds. A rejected fill may already
have changed the page and therefore still requires review.

### Material archive recovery (2026-11-02)

When a material is saved but its manifest entry cannot be completed, the attempt
now retains the file, independently attempts to record the failure and mark the
attempt for manual review, and reports any failure of those recovery writes.
Focused tests cover manifest, event and status write failures as well as normal
material preservation. A partially written manifest still requires inspection.

Newly detected material-manifest failures also set a persistent attempt flag.
The application-record list displays an actionable warning to inspect the
materials folder and manifest. Focused archive and interface tests passed.
Older records without this flag are not retrospectively audited, and absence of
the warning does not prove that an archive is complete.

### Choice-evidence classifier experiment (2026-11-02)

A separate qwen3.5:9b classification experiment matched 6 of 7 fictional
labels. It rejected both instruction-only inputs, but also rejected a valid
preference followed by a conflicting instruction. The experimental test is
resource-gated and ignored by default; it fails on that false abstention. It
is not wired into production choice advice, and does not resolve the earlier
instruction-only failure. The model was unloaded after the experiment.

### Choice model comparison and GPU reserve (2026-11-02)

The resource-gated fictional choice evaluations accept an installed model via
`WAYPOINT_CHOICE_EVAL_MODEL`; qwen3.5:9b remains the default. The fixed GPU
startup headroom is now 1.625 GiB plus 20% of model file size, down 256 MiB;
the system-RAM threshold remains unchanged. These estimates are snapshots, not
reservations. The threshold boundary test passed.

On the nine-case adversarial set, gemma4:12b-it-qat (7,151,003,754 bytes,
SHA-256 38044be4f923e5a55264ed7df4eaac2676651a905f735197c504045140c02bd3)
passed 7/9, with one wrong option and one unexpected abstention. The prior
qwen3.5:9b run passed 8/9 on the same set with one wrong option. Both chose
an option from an instruction-only answer. These are single development-set
runs, not general model rankings; no peak-memory measurement was taken. Gemma
was unloaded afterward. Before the reserve change its startup had been refused
with 9,881 MiB GPU free versus 10,104 MiB required.

### Combined choice-evidence experiment (2026-11-02)

The fictional adversarial choice test can optionally run the experimental
statement classifier first using `WAYPOINT_CHOICE_EXPERIMENTAL_GATE`. On the
nine development cases, qwen3.5:9b and gemma4:12b-it-qat each matched 8/9,
with no wrong option and one unexpected abstention. The previous ungated runs
were 8/9 with one wrong option for Qwen and 7/9 with one wrong option and one
unexpected abstention for Gemma. This is a small, non-held-out comparison;
the experimental gate is test-only, adds inference work, and is not enabled
in the application. Both models were unloaded after their runs. Separately,
Gemma matched 6/7 statement-classification labels, treating an undecided
answer as a definite statement.

### Multiple-location constraints (2026-11-02)

Location constraints now recognize semicolon-separated locations as well as
newline-separated locations. Structured listings already used semicolons, so
a matching second location could previously be excluded. A parsed fictional
listing and all 20 search-crate tests passed, including partial-location and
remote-eligibility unknown cases. This does not establish that a listing is
still open or that all geographic eligibility rules are represented.

### Incomplete structured locations (2026-11-02)

Structured listing extraction now leaves location unknown and records a review
warning when locations or remote applicant regions exceed extraction limits or
contain an unsupported entry. This avoids treating a partial list as a complete
mismatch. Fictional tests cover truncation, unsupported entries, provenance,
normal multiple locations and remote restrictions; web-app Clippy passed.
Previously saved listings are not automatically re-extracted.

### Manual listing observation time (2026-11-02)

New manually entered listings no longer claim a page observation just because
they were saved. Newly inspected listings use their validated capture timestamp;
missing, nonpositive or future timestamps remain unknown. Eight intake tests
and the observation-boundary test passed. Existing saved timestamps are not
retrospectively corrected, and inspecting an already-saved listing does not
yet refresh its observation timestamp. Recent observation is not proof that a
role is still open.

### Re-inspection observation refresh (2026-11-02)

A re-inspected saved listing can now refresh its observation timestamp when a
complete structured extraction matches the saved URL, identity, location,
remote flag, requisition and nonempty untruncated description. The update uses
a conditional database write so concurrent listing or description changes do
not get overwritten. Older observations, changed or incomplete listings,
model-only extraction and aliases do not refresh this way. The inspection
interface reports whether a refresh happened. Nine intake tests, focused
queue UI checks and web-app Clippy passed. A recent observation still does not
prove a vacancy is open, and earlier legacy timestamps are not corrected.

A disk-backed re-inspection test also confirms that the refreshed timestamp
survives reopening the workspace and that stale snapshots cannot overwrite a
newer observation or a changed location.

### Isolated wider-web discovery check (2026-11-02)

A fresh local workspace searched FreeSerp through the application for a generic
optical-engineering query. It displayed 10 unverified candidate links, including
non-job pages. Three listings were inspected: one needed an eligible model and
then remained unconfirmed, one redirected to another site and was not saved,
and one was saved with an incomplete-capture warning and application-route
review. The saved listing's reported location differed from the search query,
illustrating why search snippets cannot establish geographic fit. Qwen3.5:9b
was started through the app and unloaded after the check. No profile, files,
form answers or applications were sent. This small observation is not a recall
or precision benchmark. The empty job list now mentions wider-web search and
manual URL entry as well as boards.

### Advisory closure evidence (2026-11-02)

Local-model listing extraction now performs a bounded advisory closure check.
A positive hint must quote exact sampled page text and is shown for review; it
does not automatically mark a job closed or suppress it. Failed or malformed
checks are reported as unavailable, and a selected section is used when
available. Saved captures retain the hint with listing provenance.

An independent Crawl4AI 0.9.5+flitzzip.portable2 capture of a public listing
returned HTTP 200, 28,532 bytes of extracted markdown and SHA-256
`9cfa08c7324247bc35ffbb44ac520317f624a574d41b6570e160b7958d9fa44d`; its native
receipt remains outside Git. Qwen3.5:9b identified closure wording in a bounded
excerpt from that capture, and produced no closure hint in three fictional
non-closure cases. A prior strict-format run exposed that the model supplies
extra text with negative answers; that text is now discarded, not treated as
a closure claim. The resource-gated test passed after this adjustment. This is
not an end-to-end retest of the app's browser extraction or a reliability
benchmark; sampled text and multiple roles can still mislead the model. The
model was unloaded. Forty-eight candidate tests passed (22 ignored), both
focused UI tests and web-app Clippy passed.

### Shared-label radio advice (2026-11-03)

A resource-gated Qwen3.5:9b evaluation read a fictional form whose radio
options reference the same question labels as their group. The browser exposed
the question and distinct Yes/No options, and marked a second group with
duplicate option labels incomplete. The model selected Yes and No for explicit
positive and negative availability statements, respectively, and abstained for
an unrelated statement. No form controls were clicked. This three-case check is
not a general reliability measurement or a visual verification of a real
application; the model was unloaded after the test.

The browser can now capture a bounded common region for a radio question made
up of multiple uniquely referenced labels, without capturing the entire form.
The fictional shared-label form is 1,600 pixels tall. It refuses missing, ambiguous,
some hidden or oversized label regions rather than treating a partial crop as
complete. Six focused fictional browser tests passed, including
the shared-label capture and a hidden-label refusal. The tests establish capture
availability and refusal, not model visual accuracy or coverage of real sites.
An additional fixture checks refusal when a label's ancestor is transparent;
clipping and partial occlusion remain possible sources of visual uncertainty.
In a separate resource-gated run, Qwen3.5:9b transcribed the shared-label
question from its captured image exactly. The fictional browser was closed
before model loading, no controls were clicked, and the model was unloaded.
This is one positive observation, not a visual accuracy benchmark.

### Multipage PDF conversion and recovery (2026-11-03)

A 140-line fictional multilingual text document converted to a three-page
PDF through LibreOffice; selectable text matched the source with the isolated
PDF worker configured. An initial run without that worker produced a PDF but
could not compare its text. Conversion and template-edit results now show a
setup or Python-override recovery hint for this specific missing-tools case;
other worker errors are not exposed as document text. This does not establish
visual quality or fidelity for arbitrary documents and templates.

### Overlapping duplicate candidates (2026-11-03)

In a resource-gated fictional three-candidate check, Qwen3.5:9b flagged a
reposted role with a slightly different title and also flagged an otherwise
identical listing with a different requisition; it did not flag a junior role.
Different identifiers can indicate separate openings or syndication, so this
check does not establish the true identity of the ambiguous pair. The saved
field comparison reports requisition case differences instead of normalizing
them away, while retaining the warning that text differences are inconclusive.
The focused comparison test passed and the model was unloaded. This small
fixture is not a measurement of duplicate recall or precision.

### Emergency-contact mapping (2026-11-03)

A fictional form with Italian and Portuguese emergency-contact sections was
inspected in a local browser and evaluated by resource-gated Qwen3.5:9b. It
mapped the applicant email and sent both emergency-contact fields for review;
no fields were filled. The independent validation fallback now also recognizes
these section names and tested Spanish and German variants, even if a model
proposes an applicant value. This vocabulary is not exhaustive, and other
languages or wording can still require manual review. The model was unloaded.

The bounded recovery prompt for an incomplete form-review list now also asks
about third-party and ambiguous contact fields. In a fictional French case,
the initial mapping was deliberately simulated as wrong and its review list
omitted; Qwen3.5:9b's recovery pass flagged the contact, and the mapping was
withheld. This is one local-model observation, not a guarantee for all wording.

### Alternative qualifications in match assessment (2026-11-03)

An initial fictional local-model assessment split “Python or SQL” into two
required criteria and assigned 75 despite evidence for Python. Rubric
instructions now preserve explicit alternatives as one criterion, and the
grouped evidence check receives an alternative-specific example. In a later
resource-gated run, Python evidence supported the combined criterion; unrelated
Java evidence left it unknown. A separate three-case grouped check retained
its expected supported/unknown/supported results. These small examples do not
establish general score accuracy; grouped criteria and coverage still require
review, and the model was unloaded.

For a separate fictional language example, the first conjunction assessment
returned overlapping excerpts and no score. A generic prompt revision later
fell back to an incomplete rubric in repeated checks. With a different-language
example in the instructions, a subsequent run kept “German or French” as one
supported criterion and separated “German and French” into supported French
and unknown German. The earlier Python/SQL check also passed. This records
observed model sensitivity to prompt wording, not a repeatability guarantee.

The match badge also reports missing or unrecognized preference, requirement,
audit and qualification coverage rather than silently displaying a score with
no coverage warning. Focused badge, batch-failure and source-coverage UI tests
passed; this does not validate the underlying match model.

Grouped qualification validation now requires distinct source-quoted components
to cover each explicit list segment. A fictional response that omitted Docker
from “Python, SQL and Docker” was downgraded to unknown, while a complete
decomposition remained supported. Focused tests and local-model checks of the
earlier conjunction and alternative examples passed. This bounded text check
does not establish semantic completeness for every language or list format;
ambiguous cases can require review. Earlier assessments are marked stale.

### Search-result coverage context (2026-11-03)

Selected web-search candidates now retain a limited-coverage flag when engine
availability is unknown or degraded, a search is running or interrupted, or
the candidate limit stopped the run. The card points to the search summary;
the existing selection notice still asks the user to reselect for later reports.
Four focused search and selection UI tests passed. This does not establish
provider completeness or verify any listing.

Retries now seed their deduplication and candidate budget from retained search
pages. Repeated URLs can still contribute provider reports, while earlier and
